Feedback stabilization of Convective Brinkman-Forchheimer Extended Darcy equations 1,2,3 Department of Mathematics, Indian Institute of Technology Roorkee-IIT Roorkee, Haridwar Highway, Roorkee, Uttarakhand 247667, INDIA. e-mail: Manil T. Mohan: maniltmohan@ma.iitr.ac.in, maniltmohan@gmail.com. e-mail: Kush Kinra: kkinra@ma.iitr.ac.in. e-mail: Sagar Gautam: sagar_g@ma.iitr.ac.in. * Corresponding author. Key words: Convective Brinkman-Forchheimer extended Darcy equations, torus, monotone operators, stabilization, feedback control. Mathematics Subject Classification (2020): Primary 49J20, 49N35, 93D15; Secondary 35Q35, 76D03.
Sagar Gautam 1 , Kush Kinra 2 and Manil T. Mohan 3*

Abstract
. In this article, the following controlled convective Brinkman-Forchheimer extended Darcy (CBFeD) system is considered in a d d -dimensional torus: ∂ 𝒚 ∂ t − μ Δ 𝒚 + ( 𝒚 ⋅ ∇ ) 𝒚 + α 𝒚 + β | 𝒚 | r − 1 𝒚 + γ | 𝒚 | q − 1 𝒚 + ∇ p = 𝒈 + 𝒖 , ∇ ⋅ 𝒚 = 0 , \frac{\partial\boldsymbol{y}}{\partial t}-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{y}+(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+\beta|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}+\gamma|\boldsymbol{y}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}+\nabla p=\boldsymbol{g}+\boldsymbol{u},\ \nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{y}=0, where d ∈ { 2 , 3 } d\in\{2,3\} , μ , α , β > 0 \mu,\alpha,\beta>0 , γ ∈ ℝ \gamma\in\mathbb{R} , r , q ∈ [ 1 , ∞ ) r,q\in[1,\infty) with r > q ≥ 1 r>q\geq 1 . We prove the exponential stabilization of CBFeD system by finite- and infinite-dimensional feedback controllers. The solvability of the controlled problem is achieved by using the abstract theory of m m -accretive operators and density arguments. As an application of the above solvability result, by using infinite-dimensional feedback controllers, we demonstrate exponential stability results such that the solution preserves an invariance condition for a given closed and convex set. By utilizing the unique continuation property of controllability for finite-dimensional systems, we construct a finite-dimensional feedback controller which exponentially stabilizes CBFeD system locally, where the control is localized in a smaller subdomain. Furthermore, we establish the local exponential stability of CBFeD system via proportional controllers.



1.
Introduction

The mathematical modeling and analysis of fluid flows through porous media is an active research field, as it covers some challenging problems in engineering and applied sciences. A porous medium or a porous material is defined as a solid (often called a matrix) permeated by an interconnected network of pores (voids) filled with a fluid (e.g., air or water). We consider a porous medium that is fully saturated with a fluid (cf.
[
69
]
). Darcy’s law is the subject of a significant amount of research in the porous media. It is an empirical law which describes the linear relationship between fluid flow rate and pressure gradient. Mathematically, it says that
\boldsymbol{y}_{f}=-\frac{k}{\nu}\nabla p,
where
\boldsymbol{y}_{f}
is the Darcy velocity,
k
is the permeability of the fluid,
\nu>0
is the dynamic viscosity and
p
is the pressure in the fluid flow (cf.
[
52
]
). Darcy’s law is valid for the fluid flows with low Reynolds number (for example, oil reservoir and ground water flow). Nevertheless, nonlinearities appear with increasing Reynolds numbers, making it unreliable to describe fluid flow through porous media. For example fluid flow with high velocity, molecular and ionic effect, real fluids having non-Newtonian behavior (such as polymers and suspensions), compressible flow (such as high pressure gas reservoirs), etc. Forchheimer proposed a modification for the fluid flows in porous media with high Reynolds number (for example, flow through packed beds) which suggests that Darcy’s law still applicable but with an additional nonlinear term in order to account the pressure gradient. The Darcy’s Forchheimer law describes the relationship between the pressure gradient and flow rate in a nonlinear manner at sufficiently high flow rates, that is,
\nabla p=-\frac{\nu}{k}\boldsymbol{y}_{f}-\gamma\rho_{f}|\boldsymbol{y}_{f}|\boldsymbol{y}_{f},
where
\boldsymbol{y}_{f}
is the Forchheimer velocity,
\gamma>0
is the Forchheimer coefficient and
\rho_{f}
is the density (cf.
[
52
]
). However, the quadratic nonlinearity in the Forchheimer equation can be further generalized to incorporate some additional nonlinear terms from the mathematical point of view. Such a generalization, which takes into account the effects of both viscous resistance and inertial resistance, is called Brinkman-Forchheimer equations. These equations are used in several real world phenomena, for example, theory of non-Newtonian fluids (cf.
[
66
]
) and theory of tidal dynamics (cf.
[
39
,
51
,
56
]
), etc. By adding the convective term
(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}
, coming from the Navier-Stokes equations (NSE) to the Brinkman-Forchheimer equations, we obtain the convective-Brinkman Forchheimer equations (CBF). In this work, we consider the further generalization of CBF equations by taking into account the pumping term which also having similar nonlinearity as in the CBF equations but with opposite sign.

1.1.
The Model

For
\mathrm{L}>0
, let us consider a
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{L}\mathbb{Z}}}}\right)^{d}
(
d=2,3
), which is identified with the
d
-dimensional cube
[0,\mathrm{L}]^{d}
(see
[
40
, Chapter 3]
). In this article, we discuss the stabilization of the mathematical generalizaion of CBF equations, known as
convective Brinkman-Forchheimer extended Darcy (CBFeD) equations
(cf.
[
52
]
) on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
through feedback controllers. The CBFeD equations describe the motion of incompressible fluid flows in a saturated porous medium (
[
52
]
). In this work, we consider the following controlled CBFeD equations on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
:

(1.1)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\partial\boldsymbol{y}}{\partial t}-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{y}+(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+\beta|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}+\gamma|\boldsymbol{y}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}+\nabla p&=\boldsymbol{g}+\boldsymbol{u},\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times(0,\infty),\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{y}&=0,\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times(0,\infty),\\
\boldsymbol{y}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d},\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
\boldsymbol{y}(x,t):\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
p(x,t):\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
and
\boldsymbol{g}(x,t):\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
represent the velocity field, pressure field and external forcing, respectively, at time
t
and position
x
. The distributed control acting on the system is denoted by
\boldsymbol{u}(\cdot)
. Moreover,
\boldsymbol{y}(\cdot,\cdot)
,
p(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\boldsymbol{g}(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfy the following periodic conditions:

(1.2)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}(x+\mathrm{L}e_{i},\cdot)=\boldsymbol{y}(x,\cdot),\ p(x+\mathrm{L}e_{i},\cdot)=p(x,\cdot)\ \text{and}\ \boldsymbol{g}(x+\mathrm{L}e_{i},\cdot)=\boldsymbol{g}(x,\cdot),

for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\},
where
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
is the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
The constant
\mu>0
denotes the
Brinkman coefficient
(effective viscosity). The constants
\alpha,\beta>0
are due to Darcy-Forchheimer law and we call
\alpha
and
\beta
as
Darcy
(permeability of the porous medium) and
Forchheimer
(proportional to the porosity of the material) coefficients, respectively. The nonlinear term
\gamma|\boldsymbol{y}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}
appearing in (
1.1
) serves as damping for
\gamma>0
, and as pumping for
\gamma<0
. The parameters
r,q\in[1,\infty)
with
r>q
are known as absorption and pumping exponents, respectively. The exponent
r=3
is known as critical exponent. Note that the system (
1.1
) has same scaling as NSE (see for instance
[
41
]
) only when
\alpha=\gamma=0
and
r=3
. It can be easily seen that, for
\alpha=\beta=\gamma=0
, the system (
1.1
) reduces to the classical NSE. Furthermore, we refer the case
r<3
as
subcritical
and
r>3
as
supercritical
(or fast growing nonlinearities). We refer to
[
59
,
68
]
for a discussion on the formulation, validity, and limitations of Brinkman-Forchheimer extended Darcy’s equations.

1.2.
Stabilization

In the literature, the mathematical theory of stabilization of equilibrium solution to Newtonian fluid flows is extensively used as a principal tool to eliminate or attenuate the turbulence (cf.
[
16
]
). Let us consider the following stationary system corresponding to the CBFeD system (
1.1
):

(1.3)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} -\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\beta|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\gamma|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}+\nabla p_{e}&=\boldsymbol{g}_{e},&\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d},\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{y}_{e}&=0,&\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

The solvability of the system (
1.3
) has been discussed in Appendix
B
. In this work, we are interested in the exponential stabilization of the stationary (or equilibrium) solution
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
to the system (
1.1
) by using infinite and finite-dimensional feedback controllers such that
\boldsymbol{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
satisfies some invariance condition for all
t\geq 0
. We say that the controller
\boldsymbol{u}(\cdot)
exponentially stabilizes the stationary solution
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
if there is a “solution”
\boldsymbol{y}(\cdot)
of the system (
1.1
) such that it satisfies

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}e^{\delta t}\|\boldsymbol{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}({\mathbb{T}^{d}})}=0,\text{ for some }\delta>0.

By the substitution
\boldsymbol{y}(\cdot)
to
\boldsymbol{y}(\cdot)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
, one can reduce the problem of stabilization of
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
into the null stabilization of the following system:

(1.4)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\partial\boldsymbol{y}}{\partial t}-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{y}+((\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}&+\beta(|\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-1}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\\
+\gamma(|\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{q-1}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\nabla(p-p_{e})&=\boldsymbol{g}-\boldsymbol{g}_{e}+\boldsymbol{u},\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times(0,\infty),\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{y}&=0,\ \hskip 45.5244pt\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times(0,\infty).\end{aligned}}}\right.

Keeping these goals in mind, we take control
\boldsymbol{u}(\cdot)\in\Xi+\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
and consider the following inclusion problem associated with (
1.4
):

(1.5)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\partial\boldsymbol{y}}{\partial t}-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{y}+((\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+&\beta(|\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-1}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\\
+\gamma(|\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{q-1}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\nabla(p-p_{e})+\Xi\boldsymbol{y}+\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{y})&\ni\boldsymbol{g}-\boldsymbol{g}_{e},\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times(0,\infty),\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{y}&=0,\ \hskip 24.18483pt\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times(0,\infty),\\
\boldsymbol{y}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\hskip 23.04674pt\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d},\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
, the indicator function of a closed and convex set
\mathcal{K}
satisfying Hypothesis
3.1
below, is a lower semicontinuoius proper convex function. The subdifferential of the indicator function, that is,
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
is a maximal monotone operator and it is given by (
3.3
). Also,
\Xi:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is a continuous linear operator, and the choice of
\Xi
will be made precise later. The subdifferential of a lower semicontinuous proper convex function is a maximal monotone susbet of
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and it connects the bridge between the theory of nonlinear maximal monotone operators and convex analysis. We refer to
[
12
,
13
]
, for basic properties and concepts pertaining subdifferentials.

1.3.
Literature survey

Due to Leary and Hopf (cf.
[
43
,
45
]
), the existence of at least one weak solution of 3D NSE is known. But the uniqueness is still a challenging open problem for the mathematical community. Therefore, several mathematicians came up with various modifications of 3D NSE (cf.
[
4
,
5
,
31
,
44
,
71
]
, etc.). The authors in
[
4
,
5
]
introduced the NSE modified with an absorption term
\beta|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}
with
r>0
and proved the existence of weak solution for any
d\geq 2
and uniqueness for
d=2
. In the literature, modification of the classical NSE with the damping term
\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+\beta|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}
is known as the
convective Brinkman-Forchheimer equations
(cf.
[
38
,
41
,
54
]
, etc.). In
[
52
]
, the authors considered the classical 3D NSE with damping
\beta|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}
as well as pumping
\gamma|\boldsymbol{y}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}
(without linear damping
\alpha\boldsymbol{y}
) and established the existence of weak solutions for
r>q
and uniqueness for
r>3
. Moreover, they demonstrated the global existence of a unique strong solution for
r>3
with the initial data in
\mathbb{H}^{1}
. Similar to 3D NSE, the existence of a unique global (in time) weak solution of 3D CBFeD with
r\in[1,3)
(for any
\beta,\mu>0
) and
r=3
(for
2\beta\mu<1
) is also an open problem.

The monotone operators are a class of nonlinear operators which provide a broad framework for the analysis of infinite-dimensional problems. Of special interest are the so-called
maximal monotone operators
(or
m
-accretive operators
on Hilbert spaces identified with its own dual), which exhibit remarkable surjectivity properties. Further, for inclusion problems, once the operator becomes maximal, then one can use the Yosida approximation scheme, which is an important regularization technique for solving evolution systems (see
[
12
,
30
]
, etc.).

Control theory plays a cruiciual role in stabilization of a differential system around a specific orbit using a feedback control. One of the main problems in controllability is to find an appropriate control which steers from an arbitrary initial state to an arbitrary final (desired) state. Even though the controllabilty problem is quite satisfactory from the mathematical point of view, most of the control problems in applications require the control of closed-loop form, as they are stable under perturbations (cf.
[
33
, Part 3]
). By a closed-loop control, we mean at each instant, the control is a function of the state. In the stability problems, our main concern is to show that the solution to a given evolution equation approaches to an equilibrium solution as the time approaches to infinity (cf.
[
11
,
17
,
22
]
, etc.). One of the main results obtained in this direction is that the equilibrium solutions to NSE can be exponentially stabilized by finite- or infinite-dimensional feedback controllers with support in the interior of the domain or on the boundary (see
[
14
,
15
,
16
]
, etc.). The author in
[
46
]
established the global stabilization of 2D and 3D NSE with weak intial data by using a feedback control distributed on the whole space. Further they discussed the local stability results also by using a finite-dimensional feedback control distributed on the subdomain (see
[
47
,
48
]
). The local exponential stabilization for incompressible 2D NSE with Navier slip boundary conditions was established in
[
50
]
. They constructed a feedback control to stabilize the linearized system about an equilibrium point which helps to stabilize nonlinear system locally by using the solution of the Lyapunov equation. The similar problem in the context of 3D NSE was studied by V. Barbu in
[
20
]
, where a spectral decomposition method had been used. The feedback stabilization of 2D and 3D NSE in bounded domains by using a state decomposition technique is discussed in
[
24
]
. A local exponential stabilization for 3D NSE via finite-dimensional feedback controls by using observability inequality and the regularizing property for the corresponding linearized equation is examined in
[
26
]
. The authors in
[
8
]
provides a general framework for the stabilization of a class of nonlinear equations by using finite-dimensional feedback controllers and its applications to stabilize the equilibrium solution of 3D NSE.

The author in
[
9
]
proved an exponential stabilizability result for 2D and 3D NSE in bounded domain, where they construct the feedback controller by using Lyapunov functional method. The stability of 2D and 3D NSE with slip-curl boundary conditions is discussed in
[
23
]
by using proportional controllers which is supported locally. We refer to
[
37
]
and references therein for the stabilization in unbounded domains for 2D NSE, where the authors use the technique of impulse control. For an extensive study on boundary stabilization, we refer the readers to the articles
[
18
,
19
,
25
,
60
,
62
,
65
]
and references therein. A general approach for studying controllability of PDEs through finite-dimensional projections can be found in the articles
[
1
,
2
]
. We refer to
[
7
,
49
]
, etc. for further reading.

1.4.
Difficulties and approaches

The main advantage for considering the CBFeD system (
1.1
) in a
d
-dimensional torus is as follows: In the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, the Helmholtz-Hodge projection
\mathcal{P}
and
-\Delta
commutes (cf.
[
64
, Theorem 2.22]
). So, the equality

(1.6)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(-\Delta\boldsymbol{y}(x))\cdot|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{2}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{r-1}\/\mathrm{d}\/x+4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{r-1}{(r+1)^{2}}}}\right]\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{\frac{r+1}{2}}|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,

is quite useful in obtaining regularity results. It is also noticed in the literature that the above equality may not be useful in domains other than the whole domain or a
d
-dimensional torus (see
[
44
,
54
]
, etc. for a detailed discussion).

In order to use the abstract theory of
m
-accretive operators, the authors in
[
21
,
38
,
46
,
47
]
, etc. use the perturbation result
[
12
, Chapter II, Theorem 3.5]
. However, for
r>3,
due to the presence of fast-growing nonlinearities in (
1.1
), we are able to prove
m
-accretivity without using the perturbation result (see Step IV, Proposition
A.1
). In our previous work (
[
38
]
), we established the well-posedness of the CBF model with strong initial data (for instance,
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
) by using the abstract theory of
m
-accretive operators and we discussed its applications in control problems. In the present work, we prove the existence and uniqueness of the CBFeD systems (
1.1
) with weak initial data (for instance,
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
).

For the stability of the stationary solution corresponding to the system (
3.5
), one needs the existence of a global solution for the initial data
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}.
Due to the presence of fast growing nonlinearities
\beta|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}
in (
1.5
), we are able to prove the global existence of a unique weak solution for
r\geq 3
for
d=3
and
d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
(Sec.
4
). Obtaining the global existence of a unique weak solution for the multivalued system (
3.5
) is a challenging task, as we are dealing with a subdifferential operator. The main difficulty arises when we take the inner product with the subdifferential operator
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
. The authors in
[
38
,
46
,
47
,
48
]
addressed this difficulty by using more regularity on the initial data and exploiting the regularity of strong solutions.

In the literature of CBF equations in bounded domains, the linear damping
\alpha\boldsymbol{y}
poses no additional difficulty and therefore it is discarded from the analysis by taking
\alpha=0
. In
[
52
]
, the authors ignored the linear damping term and considered a generalization of the Darcy-Forchheimer law by taking into account the pumping term
\gamma|\boldsymbol{y}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}
with
\gamma<0
only. The linear damping term in our study cannot be avoided and must remain as it is due to a technical issue with the solvability of the stationary system corresponding to the CBFeD system (
3.5
) (non-zero average condition of velocity field, see Appendix
B
). Let us also point out that we cannot utilize Poincaré inequality in this case since the average of
\boldsymbol{y}
might not be zero. Instead, we must deal with the full
\mathbb{H}^{1}
-norm. Moreover,
0
is an eigenvalue of the Stokes operator
\mathrm{A}=-\mathcal{P}\Delta
on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
(see Subsection
2.2
) and hence
\mathrm{A}
is not an invertible operator. Therefore we have to work with the operator
\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A}
rather than
\mathrm{A}
.

The author in
[
46
]
uses a projection operator
\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
to handle the convective nonlinearity
(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}
(see
[
46
, Theorem 2.1]
). In the present article, the fast-growing nonlinearities
\beta|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}
help us to avoid this assumption, and one can tackle it by using an integration trick (see the proof of the main result in Sec.
4
). Let us now explain an additional difficulty arising in the proof of the main result. Since the initial data
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
only, one cannot obtain a convergence of the approximation of the subdifferential operator
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})(\boldsymbol{y}(\cdot))
by using the standard energy estimates. Making use of the abstract result
[
13
, Theorem 1.6]
(in particular (
4
) below), we overcome this difficulty and obtain the convergence of the subdifferential operator for all
t\in[\tau,T],
in
\mathbb{H}
, for any
\tau>0
(see more regularity estimates in Sec.
4
). Note that
\tau>0
plays a crucial role in the existence of weak solutions. The strong convergence (
4.27
) is helpful in ensuring the behavior of the solution
\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)
as
\tau\to 0^{+}
(see Def.
3.2
).

1.5.
Novelties

In this work, we are interested in a problem of finding those feedback controllers of the form
\Psi(\boldsymbol{y})
, where
\Psi
is a multivalued map such that the solution
\boldsymbol{y}(t)
of (
1.5
) reaches to the equilibrium solution
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
as
t\to\infty
and it remains inside a closed and convex set
\mathcal{K}
. That is, the solution of the system preserves some invariance condition also. These are highly applicable real life problems where one wants to find those feedback controllers which preserve some specific properties of the state under some controlled flow. One such example is that the enstrophy
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\times\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x
in a specific spatial region has to be preserved within a bound (see
[
21
]
for NSE). These feedback controllers help us to find such a bound on the evolution in time for the controlled system (
[
21
,
38
]
). Furthermore, we also look for the feedback stabilization of CBFeD system where the control takes values in a finite-dimensional space and localized in a smaller region.

Most of the feedback stabilization problems for NSE are considered for bounded domains, with control distributed over the entire domain or a subset of the domain. The authors in
[
23
]
studied the feedback stabilization for NSE by using proportional controllers with support in an open subset of a bounded domain. Following this, we prove the exponential stabilization for the CBFeD system (
1.1
), with control situated on
\Omega\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
such that
\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
is sufficiently thin (see (
C.1
) in Appendix
C
). We use the classical Rayleigh-Faber-Krahn inequality (see
[
17
, Section 5.4]
) to achieve this, which gives a lower bound for the first eigenvalue of the operator
\mu\mathrm{A}+\alpha\mathrm{I}
defined on
\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
, which is strictly greater than
0
. Note that the Stokes operator
\mathrm{A}
has
0
as an eigenvalue in our setting, so we are not able to use the Rayleigh-Faber-Krahn inequality (see (
C.11
) in Appendix
C
) for this operator.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows: In the following section, we introduce a functional setting for the theory of CBFeD model and recall some definitions and properties of linear, bilinear and nonlinear operators as well as some well-known facts. In Sec.
3
, we setup the abstract formulation (
3.5
) of (
1.1
) and state a result concerning the solvability of CBFeD equations (Theorem
3.3
). We provide the proof of Theorem
3.3
in Sec.
4
by using the abstract theory of
m
-accretive operators and density arguments. The Minty-Browder technique is exploited to pass the weak limit. Sec.
5
is devoted to the main results on the stabilization of CBFeD equations (
3.5
) by using infinite- and finite-dimensional feedback controllers (Theorem
5.1
and
5.2
). In Sec.
A
, we discuss the quasi-
m
-accretivity of the single-valued operator
\mathcal{M}(\cdot)
(Proposition
A.1
) as well the quasi-
m
-accretivity of the multivalued operator
\mathfrak{G}(\cdot)
(Proposition
A.3
). The existence and uniqueness of solutions for the stationary CBFeD system are discussed in Appendix
B
. Exponential stabilization via proportional controllers is examined in Appendix
C
.

2.
Functional settings and operators

In this section, we define some necessary function spaces which are frequently used in the rest of the paper, and linear and nonlinear operators which help us to obtain the abstract formulation of the inclusion system (
1.5
). For our analysis, we adapt the functional framework from the work
[
63
]
.

2.1.
Function spaces

Let
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the space of all infinitely differentiable functions
\boldsymbol{y}
satisfying periodic boundary conditions
\boldsymbol{y}(x+\mathrm{L}e_{i},\cdot)=\boldsymbol{y}(x,\cdot)
, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We are not assuming the zero mean condition for the velocity field unlike the case of NSE, since the absorption term
\beta|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}
does not preserve this property (see
[
52
]
). Therefore, we cannot use the well-known Poincaré inequality and we have to deal with the full
\mathbb{H}^{1}
-norm.
The Sobolev space
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{p}}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d}):=\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{p}}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the completion of
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
with respect to the
\mathbb{H}^{s}
-norm and the norm on the space
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{p}}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is given by

\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{s}_{\mathrm{p}}}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{0\leq|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq s}\|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}}}\right)^{1/2}.

It is known from
[
63
]
that the Sobolev space of periodic functions
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{p}}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is same as the space

\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{f}}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\boldsymbol{y}:\boldsymbol{y}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi ik\cdot x/\mathrm{L}},\ \overline{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}=\boldsymbol{y}_{-k},\ \|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{s}_{\mathrm{f}}}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(1+|k|^{2s})|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}|^{2}}}\right)^{1/2}<\infty}}\right\}.

We infer from
[
63
, Proposition 5.38]
that the norms
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{s}_{\mathrm{p}}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{s}_{f}}
are equivalent. Let us define

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}:=\{\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}):\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{y}=0\}.

We define the spaces
\mathbb{H}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
as the closure of
\mathcal{V}
in the Lebesgue spaces
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
p\in(2,\infty)
, respectively. We also define the space
\mathbb{V}
as the closure of
\mathcal{V}
in the Sobolev space
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, we characterize the spaces
\mathbb{H}
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
and
\mathbb{V}
with the norms

\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,\quad\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}^{p}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{p}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\ \text{ and }\ \|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{2}+|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{2})\/\mathrm{d}\/x,

respectively.
Let
(\cdot,\cdot)
denote the inner product in the Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
represent the induced duality between the spaces
\mathbb{V}
and its dual
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
as well as
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
and its dual
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p^{\prime}}
, where
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
. Note that
\mathbb{H}
can be identified with its own dual
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
. From
[
34
, Subsection 2.1]
, we have that the sum space
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p^{\prime}}
is well defined and is a Banach space with respect to the norm (see
[
57
]
also)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle:=\inf\{\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}\|_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}+\|\boldsymbol{y}_{2}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p^{\prime}}}:\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{2},\boldsymbol{y}_{1}\in\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\ \text{and}\ \boldsymbol{y}_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle=\sup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{|\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{2},\boldsymbol{f}\rangle|}{\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}}:\boldsymbol{0}\neq\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}}\right\},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}:=\max\{\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{V}},\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}\}
is a norm on the Banach space
\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
. Also the norm
\max\{\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{V}},\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}\}
is equivalent to the norms
\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}
and
\sqrt{\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}^{2}}
on the space
\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
. Furthermore, we have

(\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p^{\prime}})^{\prime}=\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}\ \text{and}\ (\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p})^{\prime}=\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p^{\prime}},

Moreover, we have the continuous embeddings

\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}\cong\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p^{\prime}},

where the embedding
\mathbb{V}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}
is compact. The following interpolation inequality is useful throughout the paper: Let
0\leq s_{1}\leq s\leq s_{2}\leq\infty
and
0\leq\theta\leq 1
be such that
\frac{1}{s}=\frac{\theta}{s_{1}}+\frac{1-\theta}{s_{2}}
. Then for
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{L}^{s_{2}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{s}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{s_{1}}}^{\theta}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{s_{2}}}^{1-\theta}.

2.2.
Linear operator

Let
\mathcal{P}_{p}:\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p},

p\in[1,\infty)
be the Helmholtz-Hodge (or Leray) projection operator (cf.
[
10
,
36
]
, etc.). Note that
\mathcal{P}_{p}
is a bounded linear operator and for
p=2
,
\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}_{2}
is an orthogonal projection (see
[
64
, Section 2.1]
). We define the Stokes operator

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}&:=-\mathcal{P}\Delta\boldsymbol{y}=-\Delta\boldsymbol{y},\;\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}),\\
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})&:=\mathbb{V}\cap{\mathbb{H}}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).\end{aligned}}}\right.

For the Fourier expansion
\boldsymbol{y}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{2\pi ik\cdot x}\boldsymbol{y}_{k},
we calculate by using Parseval’s identity

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}|^{2}\ \text{and}\ \|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=(2\pi)^{4}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|k|^{4}|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}|^{2}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|k|^{4}|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}|^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Moreover, by the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}}
, we have
\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{2}\geq\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
and hence it is immediate that both the norms are equivalent and
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A})=\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Let us define a new operator
\mathscr{A}:\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})\subset\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
by
\mathscr{A}:=\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A},
where
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
and
\mathrm{I}
is the identity operator on
\mathbb{H}
. It is clear that
\mathscr{A}
is a non-negative self-adjoint and unbounded linear operator in
\mathbb{H}
. Then, we calculate

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{A}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=(\mathscr{A}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{y},\mathscr{A}^{\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{y})=(\boldsymbol{y},\mathscr{A}\boldsymbol{y})=\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2},

which yields that
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A}^{\frac{1}{2}})=\mathbb{V}
. Moreover, the inverse
(\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A})^{-1}
is also self-adjoint. The invertibilty of
\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A}
is follows from the fact that
\mathrm{A}=-\Delta
is an
m
-accretive operator in
\mathbb{H}
(see
[
12
, Chapter 1]
), which says that
\mathrm{R}(\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A})=\mathbb{H}
. This means for every
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
,
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
, the equation
\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{f}

has a unique solution. Also, we calculate

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\langle\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle=(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{y})\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{V}},

which implies that
\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}
, that is,
\|(\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A})^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}.

Thus,
(\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A})^{-1}
maps bounded subset of
\mathbb{H}
into bounded subsets of
\mathbb{V}
. Since the embedding
\mathbb{V}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}
is compact, therefore
(\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A})^{-1}
is a compact operator on
\mathbb{H}
and thus by the spectral mapping theorem, the spectrum of
\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A}
consists of an infinite sequence of eigenvalues
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots\lambda_{k}\leq\ldots,
with
\lambda_{k}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
such that

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}\boldsymbol{w}_{k}=\lambda_{k}\boldsymbol{w}_{k},\ \ \text{for}\ k=1,2,\ldots,

where
\boldsymbol{w}_{k}
’s are the corresponding eigenfunctions in
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
which forms an orthonormal basis for
\mathbb{H}
. We can express
\boldsymbol{y}
in the Fourier expansion form as
\boldsymbol{y}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi ik\cdot x/\mathrm{L}}
or we can write

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi ik\cdot\boldsymbol{x}/\mathrm{L}}+\boldsymbol{y}_{0}:=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}+\boldsymbol{y}_{0},

where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}:=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi ik\cdot x/\mathrm{L}}
and
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}
is the element corresponding to
k=(0,\ldots,0)
.
Then from
[
64
, Chapter 6]
, we calculate

\displaystyle\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}=-\Delta\boldsymbol{y}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\mathrm{L}^{2}}}}\right)\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|k|^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi ik\cdot x/\mathrm{L}},

and therefore
\mathscr{A}\boldsymbol{y}=(\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A})\boldsymbol{y}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\mathrm{L}^{2}}}}\right)|k|^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi ik\cdot x/\mathrm{L}}.

It shows that
\boldsymbol{y}_{k}
is an eigenfunction of
\mathscr{A}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
1+\tilde{\lambda}_{k}
, where
\tilde{\lambda}_{k}=\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\mathrm{L}^{2}}|k|^{2}
. In particular, the operator
\mathscr{A}
and the Stokes operator
\mathrm{A}
have the same eigenfunctions corresponding to eigenvalues
1+\tilde{\lambda}_{k}
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{k}
, respectively. For
k=\boldsymbol{0}
and any constant vector
\boldsymbol{w}_{0},
we have
\mathscr{A}\boldsymbol{w}_{0}=(\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A})\boldsymbol{w}_{0}=\boldsymbol{w}_{0}=(1+0)\boldsymbol{w}_{0}
. Thus,
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}
is an eigenfunction of
\mathscr{A}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=1
. Note that
0
is an eigenvalue of the Stokes operator
\mathrm{A}
.

From
[
64
, Chapter 6]
, the eigenfunctions of the operator
\mathscr{A}=\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A}
on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
are given by

(2.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}_{k}^{(s)}:=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}\sin\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\pi k\cdot\frac{x}{\mathrm{L}}}}\right),\ \boldsymbol{w}_{k}^{(c)}:=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}\cos\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\pi k\cdot\frac{x}{\mathrm{L}}}}\right)\ \ \text{and}\ \boldsymbol{w}_{k}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{w}_{0},

with eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}=1+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\mathrm{L}^{2}}|k|^{2}
. We can order these eigenvalues in a non-decreasing manner such that for every
\lambda_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
, one can find a corresponding eigenvalue
\lambda_{m}
for some appropriate
m\in\mathbb{N}
, with
\lambda_{m+1}\geq\lambda_{m}
and the corresponding eigenfunction
\boldsymbol{w}_{k}=\boldsymbol{w}_{m}
(cf.
[
35
, pp. 52]
).

2.3.
Bilinear operator

Let us define the
trilinear form

b(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{R}
by

b(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w})=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\boldsymbol{y}(x)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{z}(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\boldsymbol{y}_{i}(x)\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{z}_{j}(x)}{\partial x_{i}}\boldsymbol{w}_{j}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x.

If
\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z}
are such that the linear map
b(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z},\cdot)
is continuous on
\mathbb{V}
, the corresponding element of
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
is denoted by
\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})
. We also denote
\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y})=\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{y}):=\mathcal{P}[(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}]
.
An integration by parts yields

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} b(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w})&=-b(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{z}),\ &&\text{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{V},\\
b(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z})&=0,\ &&\text{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{V}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

2.4.
Nonlinear operator

Let us define an operator
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}):=\mathcal{P}(|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y})
for
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{L}^{r+1}
. Since the projection operator
\mathcal{P}
is bounded from
\mathbb{H}^{1}
into itself (cf.
[
67
, Remark 1.6]
), the operator
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\cdot):\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}\to\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}
is well-defined and we have
\langle\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}),\boldsymbol{y}\rangle=\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}.
Moreover, for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{L}^{r+1}
, the map
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\cdot):\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}\to\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}
is Gateaux differentiable with Gateaux derivative given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathcal{P}(\boldsymbol{z}),&\text{ for }r=1,\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{P}(|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{z})+(r-1)\mathcal{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{|\boldsymbol{y}|^{3-r}}(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})}}\right),&\text{ if }\boldsymbol{y}\neq\mathbf{0},\\
\mathbf{0},&\text{ if }\boldsymbol{y}=\mathbf{0},\end{array}}}\right.&\text{ for }1<r<3,\\
\mathcal{P}(|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{z})+(r-1)\mathcal{P}(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-3}(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})),&\text{ for }r\geq 3,\end{array}}}\right.

for all
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
. For
r\geq 3
, we also have the existence of second order Gateaux derivative given by (see
[
58
, Subsection 2.4]
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})(\boldsymbol{z}\otimes\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(r-1)\mathcal{P}\{|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{z}+(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{z}+(\boldsymbol{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{y}}}\right)\},&\text{ if }\boldsymbol{y}\neq\mathbf{0},\\
\mathbf{0},&\text{ if }\boldsymbol{y}=\mathbf{0},\end{array}}}\right.&\text{ for }3<r<5,\\
(r-1)\mathcal{P}\{|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{z}+(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{z}+(\boldsymbol{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{y}}}\right)\}\\
+(r-1)(r-3)\mathcal{P}\{|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-5}(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{y}\},&\text{ for }r\geq 5,\end{array}}}\right.

for all
\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{V}\cap{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}}^{r+1}
. For all
\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{V}\cap{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}}^{r+1}
, we have (see
[
55
, Subsection 2.4]
)

(2.10)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{z}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{z})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\||\boldsymbol{z}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{z})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2^{r-1}}\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1},

for all
r\in[1,\infty)
. Moreover, we define
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}):=\mathcal{P}(|\boldsymbol{y}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y})
for
1\leq q<r
. The nonlinear operator
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\cdot)
satisfies the similar properties as
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\cdot)
.

Remark 2.1
.

From
[
57
]
, we have for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
(see
[
57
, Subsection 3.5]
)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p(r+1)}}^{r+1}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{2}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{r-1}\/\mathrm{d}\/x+C\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,

for all
p\in[2,\infty)
for
d=2
and
p=3
for
d=3
.

3.
Solvability Result

This work mainly investigates a feedback stabilization problem for the system (
1.1
) via finite- or infinite-dimensional feedback controllers. In order to achieve this, we first do the setting of a stabilization problem (see the inclusion problem (
3.5
)) and prove its well-posedness. We need the following assumption to obtain the global existence and uniqueness of weak solutions for the system (
3.5
):

Hypothesis 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{H}
be a closed and convex set such that
\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathcal{K}
and

(3.1)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{I}+\lambda\mathrm{A})^{-1}\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{K},\ \text{for all}\ \lambda>0.

We consider the indicator function
\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}:\mathbb{H}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}
(see
[
13
]
) by

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\begin{cases}{0},&\text{if }\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{K},\\
+\infty,&\text{if }\ \boldsymbol{x}\notin\mathcal{K},\end{cases}

which is a lower-semicontinuous proper convex function, whose subdifferential is given by

(3.3)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\begin{cases}\varnothing,&\text{if }\ \boldsymbol{x}\notin\mathcal{K},\\
\{\boldsymbol{0}\},&\text{if }\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{K}),\\
N_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\{\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{H}:(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})\geq 0,\ \text{for all}\ \boldsymbol{z}\in\mathcal{K}\},&\text{if }\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\mathrm{bdy}(\mathcal{K}),\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{K})
and
\mathrm{bdy}(\mathcal{K})
denote the interior and boundary of
\mathcal{K}
, respectively. Here
N_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{x})
is the well-known Clark’s normal cone to
\mathcal{K}
at
\boldsymbol{x}
. It is readily observed that
\mathrm{D}(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})=\mathcal{K}
. From
[
13
, Theorem 2.1, pp. 62]
, we infer that the subdifferential operator
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
is a maximal monotone operator in
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}
. The regularization of
\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
is given by (see
[
13
, Chapter 2, Theorem 2.2]
)

(\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{2\lambda}\|\boldsymbol{x}-\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{x})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

and its Gateaux derivative
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}-\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{x})),

where
\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathcal{K}
is the projection operator of
\boldsymbol{x}
onto
\mathcal{K}
which is equal to the resolvent
(\mathrm{I}+\lambda\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})^{-1}.
It implies that the above derivative is equal to the Yosida approximation of
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}},
that is,

(3.4)

\displaystyle(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}-(\mathrm{I}+\lambda\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{x})),\ \text{for all}\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H}.

Furthermore,
\|\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{0})\|_{\mathbb{H}}=0.
Indeed, for
\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathrm{bdy}(\mathcal{K})
, we know that

\displaystyle\|\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{0})\|_{\mathbb{H}}:=\inf\{\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}:\boldsymbol{y}\in\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{0})\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}:\boldsymbol{y}\in N_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{0})\}=0,

where we have used the fact that
\boldsymbol{0}\in N_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{0})
. On the other hand, for
\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{K})
,
\|\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{0})\|_{\mathbb{H}}=0
is obvious.

3.1.
Abstract formulation and weak solution

We first write our problem in the abstract form. For this, let us define the continuous operators
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot):\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\cdot):\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r_{i}+1}\to\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{r_{i}+1}{r_{i}}}
, where
r_{1}=r
and
r_{2}=q
, by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}):=\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\text{ and }\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{y}):=\mathcal{C}_{i}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{i}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\ \text{for}\ \ i=1,2,

where
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
is fixed. It is observed that the operators
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\cdot)
take values from
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
into
\mathbb{H}
also. We now consider the CBFeD system (
1.5
) in an abstract form perturbed by a subdifferential:

(3.5)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}(t)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}(t)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))&+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))+\mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}(t)+\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))\ni\boldsymbol{f}(t),\\
\boldsymbol{y}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\end{aligned}}}\right.

for
a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, where
\mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}:=\mathcal{P}\Xi\boldsymbol{y}
, the initial data
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
and forcing term
\boldsymbol{f}:=\mathcal{P}(\boldsymbol{g}-\boldsymbol{g}_{e})\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
. Since
\mathfrak{F}
is a continuous linear operator on
\mathbb{H}
, there exists a constant
M
such that

(3.6)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq M\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}},

for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{H}
. We are now in a position to provide the definition of solution for the system (
3.5
) in the weak sense.

Definition 3.2
.

A function
\boldsymbol{y}(\cdot)
is said to be a
weak solution
of the system (
3.5
) on the time interval
[0,T]
, with initial data
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
if

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}),

with
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(\tau,T;\mathbb{H}),
for any
\tau>0
, and there exists
\xi(t)\in\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))
for a.e.
t\in[\tau,T]
such that following equality holds:

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{t}(\boldsymbol{y}(s),\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\mu\int_{\tau}^{t}(\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\alpha\int_{\tau}^{t}(\boldsymbol{y}(s),\boldsymbol{v}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle+\int_{\tau}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(((\boldsymbol{y}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{y}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{v}(s)}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\beta\int_{\tau}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{y}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-1}(\boldsymbol{y}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{v}(s)}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle+\gamma\int_{\tau}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{y}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{q-1}(\boldsymbol{y}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{v}(s)}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\int_{\tau}^{t}(\mathfrak{F}(\boldsymbol{y}(s)),\boldsymbol{v}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\int_{\tau}^{t}(\xi(s),\boldsymbol{v}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\quad=-(\boldsymbol{y}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{v}(t))+(\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{v}(\tau))+\int_{\tau}^{t}(\boldsymbol{f}(s),\boldsymbol{v}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\int_{\tau}^{t}((\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{v}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\beta\int_{\tau}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{v}(s)}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\gamma\int_{\tau}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{v}(s)}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

for all
t\in[\tau,T]
and for all functions
\boldsymbol{v}
in the space of divergence free test functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{T}

\displaystyle:=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[\tau,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}):\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0\},

and the initial data is satisfied in the following sense:

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to 0^{+}}\|\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}=0.

Let us now state the main result of this paper regarding the solvability of the system (
3.5
).

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{H}
satisfiy Hypothesis
3.1
. Let
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathcal{K}=\overline{\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathcal{K}}^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{H}}}
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
. Then, for
d=2,3
with
r\geq 3
and
d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
, there exists a weak solution
\boldsymbol{y}(\cdot)
of the system (
3.5
) in the sense of Definition
3.2
. Furthermore, such a weak solution is unique.

We prove Theorem
3.3
in Sec.
4
.

Remark 3.4
.

The multivalued operator
\mathfrak{G}(\cdot)=\mu\mathrm{A}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)+\alpha\mathrm{I}+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\cdot)+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\cdot)+\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
is a maximal monotone operator with
\mathrm{D}(\mathfrak{G})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathcal{K}
(see Proposition
A.3
below). Since
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
is dense subspace of
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
is a closed subset of
\mathbb{H}
, therefore from
[
29
, Remark and Theorem 11, pp. 110]
, we have
\overline{\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathcal{K}}^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{H}}}=\overline{\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})}^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{H}}}\cap\mathcal{K}=\mathbb{H}\cap\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}
.

4.
Proof of Theorem
3.3

In this section, we prove the solvability result (Theorem
3.3
) of the system (
3.5
), that is, the existence and uniqueness of weak solutions of the system (
3.5
). The proof is based on the existence of unique strong solutions of some Yosida approximated system (see the system (
4.2
) below).

Let
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).
Since the embeddings
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathcal{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}
and
\mathrm{W}^{1,1}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
are dense, we obtain two sequences
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{0}^{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
and
\{\boldsymbol{f}^{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
in
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathcal{K}
and
\mathrm{W}^{1,1}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
, respectively, such that

(4.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}_{0}^{j}\to\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H}\ \text{and}\ \boldsymbol{f}^{j}\to\boldsymbol{f}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\ \mbox{ as }\ j\to\infty.

Let us consider the following Yosida approximated system:

(4.2)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))+&\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))+\mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))\\
&=\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t),\ \text{ a.e. }\ t\in[0,T],\\
\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0}^{j}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\cdot)
is the Yosida approximation
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\cdot)
of the subdifferential
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
for
\lambda=\frac{1}{j}
. Form Proposition
A.1
, we have shown that the operator
\mu\mathrm{A}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\cdot)+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}_{2}}(\cdot)+(\alpha+\kappa)\mathrm{I}
is maximal monotone in
\mathbb{H}
for sufficiently large
\kappa>0
, while the operator
\mathfrak{F}(\cdot)
is hemicontinous since it is linear and continuous from
\mathbb{H}
to
\mathbb{H}
. Thus, from
[
13
, Corollary 1.4, pp 44]
, it implies that the operator
\mu\mathrm{A}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\cdot)+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\cdot)+\mathfrak{F}(\cdot)+(\alpha+\kappa)\mathrm{I}
is maximal monotone in
\mathbb{H}.
Moreover, since the Yosida approximation
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\cdot)
is a demicontinuous and monotone operator, from
[
13
, Corollary 1.5, pp 56]
, we infer that
the operator
\mu\mathrm{A}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\cdot)+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\cdot)+\mathfrak{F}(\cdot)+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\cdot)+(\alpha+\kappa)\mathrm{I}
is maximal monotone in
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}.
Hence, we have following result for (
4.2
) (cf.
[
38
, Proposition 3.4]
):

(4.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}^{j}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))\cap\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{V}),

\boldsymbol{y}^{j}
is right differentiable,
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}
is right continuous and

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))+\mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))

(4.4)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t),\ \text{ for all }\ t\in(0,T].

Energy estimates.
Taking the inner product in the first equation of the system (
4.2
) with
\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\cdot)
and using a similar calculation as in (
A.24
), we obtain for a.e.
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2^{r}}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\leq\frac{1}{4}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+k\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
k:=M+1+\varrho_{\frac{1}{2}}+\rho_{1}+\rho_{\frac{1}{2}}.

Integrating above over
(0,t)
and applying Gronwall’s inequality, we find

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\frac{\beta}{2^{r-1}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}^{j}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right)e^{2kT},

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Therefore, we have

(4.5)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{j>0}\bigg(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\frac{\beta}{2^{r-1}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/s\bigg)\leq C,

where
C=C(\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}},\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;{\mathbb{H}})},T,k)>0
is a constant independent of
j
. From the above energy estimate, we have following weak convergences:

(4.6)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{y}^{j}&\xrightharpoonup{w^{\ast}}\ \boldsymbol{y}\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),\ \ \mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}\xrightharpoonup{w^{\ast}}\mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),\\
\boldsymbol{y}^{j}&\xrightharpoonup{w}\ \boldsymbol{y}\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}).\end{aligned}}}\right.

We rewrite (
4.2
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}^{j}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\alpha(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})

\displaystyle+\beta\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\gamma\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}

\displaystyle+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{f}^{j}+\mathscr{G}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),

where
\mathscr{G}(\cdot):=\mu\mathrm{A}+\mathcal{B}(\cdot)+\alpha\mathrm{I}+\beta\mathcal{C}_{1}(\cdot)+\gamma\mathcal{C}_{2}(\cdot)
. Let us now take the inner product with
\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\cdot)
in (
4.2
) and rearranging terms, we get for a.e.
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+\alpha\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle+((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)))+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))

\displaystyle+\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+(\mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))+(\mathscr{G}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))+\mu(\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})

\displaystyle\quad+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}).

We consider the following cases:

Case I:

r>3
for
d=2,3
.
By using the Cauchy Schwarz and Young’s inequalities and calculations similar to (
A.27
)-(
A
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{4}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\mu}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{2}{\mu}\|\mathscr{G}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{4}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(\varrho_{\frac{1}{4}}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{4M^{2}}{\mu}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{3(r+1)-2q}{3(r+1)-3q+1}}+C\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{\frac{(r+1)(r+3)}{r+7}}.

Integrating the above expression over
(\tau,t)
, for
\tau>0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\beta\int_{\tau}^{t}\||\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle+\alpha\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\frac{4C(t-\tau)}{\mu}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu(t-\tau)}{4}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2(\varrho_{\frac{1}{4}}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\frac{4M^{2}}{\mu}\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

(4.7)

\displaystyle\quad+C\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{3(r+1)-2q}{3(r+1)-3q+1}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right)^{\frac{r+3}{r+7}}(t-\tau)^{\frac{4}{r+7}},

for all
t\in[\tau,T].
Using the energy estimate (
4.5
), we find

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\beta\int_{\tau}^{t}\||\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+C(t-\tau)\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+C+C(t-\tau)^{\frac{4}{r+7}}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(t-\tau)+(t-\tau)^{\frac{4}{r+7}}+1}}\right],

where
C=C(\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}},\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;{\mathbb{H}})},\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}},T,M)>0
is a constant.
Now integrating (
4
) with respect to
\tau
over
(0,t)
, we achieve

\displaystyle t\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+t+t^{2}+t^{\frac{r+11}{r+7}}}}\right],

which along with (
4.5
) gives

(4.9)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[1+\frac{1}{t}+t+t^{\frac{4}{r+7}}}}\right],

for all
t\in[\tau,T]
, where the constant
C=C(\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}},\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;{\mathbb{H}})},\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}},T,M,\mu)>0
is independent of
j
. Therefore, from (
4
)-(
4.9
), one can conclude that for all
t\in[\tau,T]

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\beta\int_{\tau}^{t}\||\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

(4.10)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[1+\frac{1}{\tau}+T+T^{\frac{4}{r+7}}}}\right].

Case II:

d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
.

One can perform the similar calculations as we have done in (
A
)-(
A
) and then integrate over
(\tau,t)
for
\tau>0
to obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu(1-\theta)\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta-\frac{1}{2\theta\mu}}}\right)\int_{\tau}^{t}\||\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\leq C+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+C(t-\tau)+C(t-\tau)^{\frac{3}{5}}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[1+\frac{1}{\tau}+T+T^{\frac{2}{5}}}}\right],

for all
t\in[\tau,T]
and
0<\theta<1
, where we have used the energy estimates (
4.5
) for
r=3
.

Higher regularity estimates.
Let us write the first equation of the system (
4.2
) for
t+h
with
h>0
as

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h))+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h))+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h))

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{F}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t+h),

for a.e.
t\in[0,T].
Subtracting the above equation form the first equation of the system (
4.2
), taking the inner product with

\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\cdot+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\cdot)
and using the similar calculations as in (
A.4
)-(
A.9
), we derive

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

(4.11)

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\mathfrak{K}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, where
\mathfrak{K}:=M+\varrho_{\frac{1}{2}}+\rho_{1}+\rho_{\frac{1}{2}}
and the constants
M
,
\varrho_{\frac{1}{2}}
,
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{\frac{1}{2}}
are same as given in (
3.6
), (
A.4
), (
A.9
), respectively.
It is immediate from (
4
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\mathfrak{K}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}},

for a.e.
t\in[0,T].
Using the Gronwall inequality, we obtain

(4.12)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq e^{\mathfrak{K}(t-\tau)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s+h)-\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right),

for all
t\in[\tau,T].
Dividing (
4.12
) by
h
and taking limit as
h\to 0
, we find

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\mathfrak{K}T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right).

for all
t\in[\tau,T].
Now, integrating (
4.13
) with respect to
\tau
on
(0,t)
, we derive

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\frac{e^{\mathfrak{K}T}}{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\tau}}\right)+e^{\mathfrak{K}T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

which implies

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\frac{e^{\mathfrak{K}T}}{\tau}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\tau}}\right)+e^{\mathfrak{K}T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

Again we apply the Gronwall inequality to obtain

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{\mathfrak{K}T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right)\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{Te^{\mathfrak{K}T}}{\tau}}}\right),

for all
t\in[\tau,T].
For
r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
, from Remark
A.2
, one obtains for a.e.
t\in[\tau,T]

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}\right)\bigg[\||\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\mathfrak{K}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t+h)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\mathfrak{K}_{1}=M+\widetilde{\rho}_{1}+\widetilde{\rho}_{2}
. Then by performing similar calculations as we have done for
r>3
, we obtain

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/^{+}\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(e^{\mathfrak{K}_{1}T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right)\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{Te^{\mathfrak{K}_{1}T}}{\tau}}}\right),

for all
t\in[\tau,T].

Uniform bounds for operators.

Using Agmon’s and Young’s inequalities, we calculate

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{1-\frac{d}{4}}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{\frac{d}{4}}}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{1-\frac{d}{4}}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}(\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{d}{4}}+\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{d}{4}})}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2-\frac{d}{2}}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{d}{2}}+\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}(\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2})}}\right].

In view of (
4.5
) and (
4
), we obtain

(4.16)

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\tau}+t+t^{\frac{4}{r+7}}}}\right)^{2},

for all
t\in[\tau,T].
Moreover, by interpolation inequality, we infer

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}^{r+1}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{(r+3)(r+1)}{r(3r+1)}}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{3(r+1)}}^{\frac{3(r-1)(r+1)}{r(3r+1)}}.

Applying Hölder’s inequality, and using the energy estimate (
4.5
) and (
4
), we find

(4.17)

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{3(r+1)}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right)^{\frac{3(r-1)}{r(3r+1)}}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\tau}+t+t^{\frac{4}{r+7}}}}\right),

for all
t\in[\tau,T],
where we have used the fact that
\frac{3(r-1)}{r(3r+1)}<1.
Again, by interpolation inequality, we calculate

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{q+1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2q}}^{q+1}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{(q+1)(3(r+1)-2q)}{q(3(r+1)-2)}}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{3(r+1)}}^{\frac{3(r+1)(q^{2}-1)}{q(3(r+1)-2)}}.

Therefore, similar to (
4.17
), we find

(4.18)

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{q+1}{q}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\tau}+t+t^{\frac{4}{r+7}}}}\right),

for all
t\in[\tau,T]
, where we have used
\frac{3(q^{2}-1)}{q(3r+1)}<1.
Thus by using (
4.14
) (for
r>3
), (
4.15
) (for
d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
) and (
4.16
)-(
4.18
), we finally conclude from (
4
) that

(4.19)

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{t}\|(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\tau}+t+t^{\frac{4}{r+7}}}}\right)^{2},

for all
t\in[\tau,T].
Let us rewrite the system (
4.2
) as

(4.20)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))&=\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(t),\ \text{ a.e. }\ t\in[0,T],\\
\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0}^{j}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
\mathscr{F}(\cdot):=\mu\mathrm{A}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}+\alpha\mathrm{I}+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\cdot)+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\cdot)+\mathfrak{F}
. From (
4.16
)-(
4.20
), we find

(4.21)

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{t}\|\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s\leq C.

From (
4.19
)-(
4.21
), making use of the Banach-Alaoglu theorem, we have the following weak convergences as
j\to\infty
:

(4.22)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j})&\xrightharpoonup{w}\mathscr{F}_{0}\ &&\text{in}\ \mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(\tau,T;\mathbb{H})\\
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j})&\xrightharpoonup{w}\phi\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(\tau,T;\mathbb{H}),\\
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}^{j}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}&\xrightharpoonup{w}\ \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(\tau,T;\mathbb{H}).\end{aligned}}}\right.

Strong convergences
.

Let
\{\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}_{0}\}_{k\geq 1}
and
\{\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}_{0}\}_{l\geq 1}
be two subsequences of
\{\boldsymbol{y}^{j}_{0}\}_{j\geq 1}
such that (
4.2
) holds for
\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(\cdot)
and
\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(\cdot)
with the initial data
