\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(0)=\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(0)=\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}_{0}
, respectively and external forcing
\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{k}}(\cdot)
and
\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{l}}(\cdot)
, respectively, where
\{\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{k}}(\cdot)\}_{k\geq 1}
and
\{\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{l}}(\cdot)\}_{l\geq 1}
are two subsequences of
\{\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(\cdot)\}_{j\geq 1}
such that
\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{k}}\to\boldsymbol{f}
and
\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{k}}\to\boldsymbol{f}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).
Note that both
\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}
and
\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}
have the regularity given in (
4.3
). Then from (
4.1
), we have

(4.23)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}_{0}\to\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\ \text{ and }\ \boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}_{0}\to\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H}.

Moreover, for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t))+\mu(\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+\alpha(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t))

\displaystyle+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j_{k}}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t))-(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j_{l}}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{l}}(t)-(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))

\displaystyle\quad-\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))-\mathfrak{F}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)).

Taking the inner product with
\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(\cdot)
, using the monotonicity of
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})(\cdot)
and performing the calculations as we have done in (
A.4
)-(
A.9
) leads to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

(4.24)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{k}}-\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{l}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathfrak{a}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\mathfrak{a}>0
is a constant independent of
j_{k}
and
j_{l}
. Using (
2.10
), we find from (
4
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t))\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2^{r}}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

(4.25)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{l}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathfrak{a}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. Now integrating (
4
) over
(0,t)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(s)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(s))\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\frac{\beta}{2^{r-1}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(s)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(s)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(0)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{k}}(s)-\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{l}}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+2\mathfrak{a}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(s)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Applying the Gronwall inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(s)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(s))\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\frac{\beta}{2^{r-1}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(s)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(s)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

(4.26)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(0)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{l}}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{k}}(s)-\boldsymbol{f}^{j_{l}}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right),

for all
t\in[0,T]
and for all
j,k\in\mathbb{N}.
This shows that the sequence
\{\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)\}
for all
t\in[0,T]
is a uniformly Cauchy sequence in
\mathbb{H}
and since
\mathbb{H}
is complete, we obtain the following uniform convergence:

(4.27)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(t)\to\boldsymbol{y}(t)\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H}\ \mbox{ for all }\ t\in[0,T].

Since
\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
, the uniform convergence implies
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
. By similar reasoning as above, we obtain from (
4
) that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}\to\boldsymbol{y}\ \ \text{in}\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\ \text{ and }\ \boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}\to\boldsymbol{y}\ \ \text{in}\ \mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}).

Note that from (
4.27
), by continuity, we have following results:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(0)\to\boldsymbol{y}(0)\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H}\ \text{ and }\ \boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(T)\to\boldsymbol{y}(T)\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H}.

Let us now prove that
\phi(t)\in\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))
for a.e.
t\in[\tau,T]
, for any
\tau>0
.
From
[
13
, Proposition 1.4, part(i), pp. 52]
, we know that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}
is nonexpansive, that is, Lipschitz continuous with the Lipschitz constant
1
and from
[
13
, Proposition 1.3, part (iii), pp. 49]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))-\boldsymbol{y}(t)\bigg\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))\bigg\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\quad+2\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))-\boldsymbol{y}(t)\bigg\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+2\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))-\boldsymbol{y}(t)\bigg\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\to 0\ \text{ as }\ j\to\infty,

so that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j})\to\boldsymbol{y}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))\to\boldsymbol{y}(t)
, for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
in
\mathbb{H}
(along a subsequence). Moreover, the maximal monotone operator
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
is weak-strong and strong-weak closed in
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}
(see
[
13
, Proposition 1.1, part (i), pp. 37]
and
[
61
, Proposition 1.7]
), which means that if

\displaystyle(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j})

\displaystyle\in\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j})}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{I}+\frac{1}{j}\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j})

\displaystyle\to\boldsymbol{y}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\ \text{ and }(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j})\xrightharpoonup{w}\ \phi\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(\tau,T;\mathbb{H}),

then
\phi\in\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{y})
for a.e.
t\in[\tau,T]
in
\mathbb{H}
.

Passing limit via Minty-Browder trick
.
We pass weak limit in (
4.20
) to obtain

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mathscr{F}_{0}+\phi(t)&=\boldsymbol{f}(t),\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(\tau,T;\mathbb{H})\\
\boldsymbol{y}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\end{aligned}}}\right.

for some
\phi\in\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{y})
. Our aim is to now show that
\mathscr{F}_{0}(t)=\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))
for a.e.
t\in[\tau,T]
. Using (
4.22
), (
4.27
) and the fact that
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
is weak-strong and strong-weak closed, we calculate (along a subsequence)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\tau}^{t}((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s-\int_{\tau}^{t}(\phi(s),\boldsymbol{y}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\tau}^{t}((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))-\phi(s),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)-\boldsymbol{y}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\tau}^{t}((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))-\phi(s),\boldsymbol{y}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\tau}^{t}(\phi(s),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)-\boldsymbol{y}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right|

\displaystyle\to 0\ \ \text{ as }\ j\to\infty,

for all
t\in[\tau,T]
and
\phi\in\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{y})
. Since the map
t\mapsto\|\boldsymbol{y}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
is absolutely continuous (cf. (
4.6
) and (
4.22
)) on the interval
[\tau,T]
with
\tau>0
, we have the following energy equality:

(4.28)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\int_{\tau}^{t}(\mathscr{F}_{0}(s)-\boldsymbol{f}(s),\boldsymbol{y}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s+2\int_{\tau}^{t}(\phi(s),\boldsymbol{y}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s=\|\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for all
t\in[\tau,T]
with
\tau>0
. Taking the inner product with
\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\cdot)
in the first equation of the system (
4.20
), we derive the following energy equality:

(4.29)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\int_{\tau}^{t}(\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))-\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s+2\int_{\tau}^{t}((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s=\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for all
t\in[\tau,T]
with
\tau>0
.
Taking the limit supremum in (
4.29
), we obtain

\displaystyle\limsup\limits_{j\to\infty}\int_{\tau}^{t}(\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle=\limsup\limits_{j\to\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{\tau}^{t}(\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s-\int_{\tau}^{t}((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\limsup\limits_{j\to\infty}\int_{\tau}^{t}(\boldsymbol{f}^{j}(s),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s-\liminf\limits_{j\to\infty}\int_{\tau}^{t}((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\frac{1}{j}}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s)),\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s

(4.30)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{\tau}^{t}(\boldsymbol{f}(s),\boldsymbol{y}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s-\int_{\tau}^{t}(\phi(s),\boldsymbol{y}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s=\int_{\tau}^{t}(\mathscr{F}_{0}(s),\boldsymbol{y}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

for all
t\in[\tau,T]
, where we have used (
4.28
) in the final equality. One can easily see from Proposition
A.1
that the operator
\mathscr{F}(\cdot)
is quasi-
m
-accretive. Therefore, we write

(4.31)

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{T}(\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))-\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)),\boldsymbol{v}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t))\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\mathfrak{K}\int_{\tau}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}^{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\geq 0,

where
\mathfrak{K}
is same as in (
4
). On taking the limit supremum in (
4.31
) and using (
4
), we obtain

(4.32)

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{T}(\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))-\mathscr{F}_{0}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}(t))\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\mathfrak{K}\int_{\tau}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\geq 0,

for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
. Substituting
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{y}+\lambda\boldsymbol{w}
(for
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
) in (
4.32
) and dividing by
\lambda
result to

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{T}(\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{y}(t)+\lambda\boldsymbol{w}(t))-\mathscr{F}_{0}(t),\boldsymbol{w}(t))\/\mathrm{d}\/t\geq 0.

Since
\mathscr{F}(\cdot)
is hemicontinuous, we conclude that
\mathscr{F}_{0}(t)=\mathscr{F}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))
, for a.e.
t\in[\tau,T]
in
\mathbb{H}
.

Initial data
.

We want to show that

(4.33)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\tau\to 0^{+}}\|\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}=0.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given. Note that from (
4.27
), one can conclude by the continuity that
\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(0)\to\boldsymbol{y}(0)
in
\mathbb{H}
. This means, there is a sufficiently large
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(0)-\boldsymbol{y}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}<\frac{\epsilon}{3}\ \text{ for all }\ k\geq k_{1}.

Similarly, from (
4.23
), there exists sufficiently large
k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(0)-\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}<\frac{\epsilon}{3}\ \text{ for all }\ k\geq k_{2}.

Using the triangle inequality, one can calculate

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{y}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(0)-\boldsymbol{y}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{y}^{j_{k}}(0)-\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}.

Since
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
(cf. (
4.27
)), it is clear that the first term in the right hand side of above inequality approaches to
0
as
\tau\to 0
from right. Therefore, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists some
\delta>0
such that for all
k\geq k_{0}:=\max\{k_{1},k_{2}\}
, we have following implication from above inequality

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}<\epsilon\ \text{ if }\ 0<\tau<\delta,

which completes the proof of (
4.33
).

Uniqueness
.
Assume that
\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(\cdot)
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(\cdot)
be any two solutions of the system (
4.2
) satisfying the initial data
\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(0)=\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(0)=\boldsymbol{y}_{0}
, respectively.
Then one can obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t))+\mu(\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t))+\alpha(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t))

\displaystyle+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+\xi(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t))-\xi(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t))

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))-\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))-\mathfrak{F}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t)),

for a.e.
t\in[\tau,T]
. Taking the inner product with
\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(\cdot)
and doing similar calculations as in (
4
)-(
4
) imply that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\mu\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t))\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2^{r}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{a}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for a.e.
t\in[\tau,T].

Thus by the Gronwall inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{H}}e^{\mathfrak{a}(t-\tau)}\leq(\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{H}})e^{\mathfrak{a}(t-\tau)}.

In view of (
4.33
), we complete the proof of uniqueness.

5.
Stability

In this section, we discuss stabilization of CBFeD system by means of finite- and infinite-dimensional feedback controllers.

5.1.
Infinite-dimensional feedback controllers

Let us consider the following controlled CBFeD system for a.e.
t>0
:

(5.1)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}(t)+\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}(t)+\beta\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))+\gamma\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}(t))&=\boldsymbol{f}_{e}+\mathbf{U}(t),\\
\boldsymbol{y}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
\mathbf{U}(\cdot)
is a distributed control acting on the whole torus. Let
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
be the steady state (equilibrium) solution of the stationary equation

(5.2)

\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}(t)+\beta\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\gamma\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})=\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d},

whose solvability is discussed in Appendix
B
(see Theorem
B.1
below). Let
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{H}
satisfy Hypothesis
3.1
. Our aim is to find those feedback controllers which stabilize the equlibrium solution
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
of (
5.1
) exponentially, under some invariance condition. So, we would like to find a feedback controller, say
\mathbf{U}(\cdot)
, such that following conditions meet:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}e^{\delta t}\|\boldsymbol{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}=0,\ \text{for some}\ \delta>0,

and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\in\mathcal{K}\ \text{for all}\ t\in[0,T].

Let us set
\boldsymbol{z}(\cdot)=\boldsymbol{y}(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
and we take
\mathbf{U}(t)\in-\theta\boldsymbol{z}(t)-(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})(\boldsymbol{z}(t))
, for some
\theta>0
. Then from (
5.1
) and (
5.2
), we consider the following inclusion problem for (
5.1
) for a.e.
t>0
:

(5.3)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\alpha\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\theta\boldsymbol{z}(t)+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})(\boldsymbol{z}(t))&\ni\boldsymbol{0},\\
\boldsymbol{z}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

Let us now prove the following global stability result:

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
satisfy Hypothesis
3.1
. Let us suppose
\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\in\mathbb{H}
is given and
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
satisfies (
5.2
). Moreover, we also assume that
\boldsymbol{z}(0)\in\mathcal{K}
. Then for sufficiently large
\theta>0
(to be specified later), the problem (
5.3
) has a unique weak solution for
r\geq 3
satisfying

(5.4)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})\ \text{ with }\ \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(\tau,T;\mathbb{H}),

for any
0<\tau<T
. Furthermore, there exists an
\delta=\delta(\theta)>0
such that the following global exponential stability condition holds:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}e^{\delta t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}=0.

Also,
\boldsymbol{z}(t)\in\mathcal{K}
for all
t\in[0,T].

Proof.

For the time being, we assume that
\theta>0
be a fixed constant. One can apply main Theorem
3.3
for
\boldsymbol{f}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
\mathfrak{F}=\theta\mathrm{I}
to ensure the existence of a unique weak solution of the system (
5.3
) having the regularity (
5.4
).

Let us now prove exponential stability. For this purpose, we take the inner product with
\boldsymbol{z}(\cdot)
in (
5.3
) and use previous calculations (see Proposition
A.1
) to get for a.e.
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}{4}}}\right)\||\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}}\right)\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq-(\theta+\alpha-\mathfrak{C})\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\mathfrak{C}:=\varrho_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}+\rho_{\varepsilon}
and we have used the monotonicity of
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
with
\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathcal{K}.
This implies

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq-2(\theta+\alpha-\mathfrak{C})\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. Let us choose
\theta>0
be sufficiently large such that
\delta_{1}=\theta+\alpha-\mathfrak{C}>0.
Using the Gronwall inequality, we have for all
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq e^{-2\delta_{1}t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

We choose
\delta\in(0,\delta_{1})
such that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}e^{\delta t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}=0,

which proves that the solutions of (
5.3
) are exponentially stabilizable. In view of Hypothesis
3.1
, Remark
3.4
and
[
27
, Corollary 2.54, pp. 56]
, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{z}(t)\in\mathcal{K}
for all
t\in[0,T].

∎

5.2.
Finite-dimensional compactly supported stabilizing feedback controllers

Let us now discuss the stability problem via finite-dimensional controllers which are compactly supported. Here the controllers are situated in smaller parts of the domain and taking values in some finite-dimensional subspace spanned by the eigenfunctions of the Stokes operator. Since the second order derivatives of the nonlinear operators are involved, we restrict ourselves to
r=3
with
\gamma=0,
and
r>3
and
q\in[3,r)
with
\gamma<0
in this subsection.

Let
\{\boldsymbol{w}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the eigenfunctions of the Stokes operator
\mathrm{A}
. We consider

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}:=\mathrm{linspan}\{\boldsymbol{w}_{k}:k=1,\ldots,n\}\subset\mathbb{H}.

It is clear that
\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}
is a closed and convex set. Also, since
(\mathrm{I}+\lambda\mathrm{A})
is invertible and
\mathcal{K}
is finite-dimensional, therefore, it satisfies the condition (
3.1
) of Hypothesis
3.1
. Moreover, we re-arrange the sequence
\{\boldsymbol{w}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}
of eigenfunctions of the Stokes operator
\mathrm{A}
in such a way that the first
k
eigenfunctions are exactly in the same position as in the generators of
\mathcal{K}.
Then, for any
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{H}
, we can write
\boldsymbol{y}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}y_{k}\boldsymbol{w}_{k},
where
y_{k}=(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})\in\mathbb{R}
. We denote
\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}},
the orthogonal projection (finite-dimensional) in
\mathbb{H}
onto
\mathcal{K}.
Then one may write
\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{y}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}y_{k}\boldsymbol{w}_{k}\in\mathcal{K}.
Note that
\text{int}(\mathcal{K})=\varnothing
, as
\mathcal{K}
is a proper subspace of
\mathbb{H}
.

We consider a non-empty open subset
\omega\subset\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Our aim is now to construct a finite-dimensional feedback controller
\mathbf{U}(\cdot)
which is supported in the cylinder
\omega\times(0,\infty)
such that the equilibrium solution
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
of the system (
5.1
) are locally exponential stabilizable. Given
\omega\subset\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
, the application
m:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega)\to\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that

\displaystyle(m\boldsymbol{y})(x):=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{y}(x),\ &x\in\omega,\\
0,\ &x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus\omega,\end{cases}

extends the functions in the subdomain
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega)
to the whole space
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Let
\mathrm{R}:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega)
be a linear and continuous operator.

Similar to (
5.3
), we choose
\mathbf{U}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{P}m\mathrm{R}(\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{z}(\cdot))-\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{z}(\cdot))
and we consider the following problem for a.e.
t>0
:

(5.6)

\hskip-11.38109pt\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\alpha\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))-\mathcal{P}m\mathrm{R}(\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{z}(t))&\ni\boldsymbol{0},\\
\boldsymbol{z}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
be the solution of (
5.2
). We now state the following local stability result:

Theorem 5.2
.

For
\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\in\mathbb{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
be the set given by (
5.5
). Also, assume that
\boldsymbol{z}(0)\in\mathcal{K}
with
\|\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}<\rho,
for some
\rho>0
. Then for every
\delta>0
, there exists a linear continuous operator
\mathrm{R}:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega)
so that the problem (
5.6
) has a unique weak solution
\boldsymbol{z}(\cdot)
for
r>3
and
d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
, satisfying

(5.7)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})\ \ \text{ with }\ \ \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(\tau,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime}),

for any
0<\tau<T
. Furthermore, the following local exponential stability condition holds:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}e^{\delta t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}=0.

Also,
\boldsymbol{z}(t)\in\mathcal{K},
for all
t\in[0,T].

Proof.

We prove the theorem in the following steps:

Step 1.

The finite-dimensional system:

For any linear continuous operator
\mathrm{R}
, the operator
\mathcal{P}m\mathrm{R}\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
is linear continuous as it is the composition of linear continuous operators. Thus from Theorem
3.3
, with
\mathfrak{F}=-\mathcal{P}m\mathrm{R}\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, one can conclude that the system (
5.6
) has a unique weak solution with required regularity given in (
5.7
). Moreover, we have
\boldsymbol{z}(t)\in\mathcal{K}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Now we only need to show the existence of such a
stabilizing operator

\mathrm{R}
. For this, let us first consider the following system for a.e.
t>0
:

(5.8)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\alpha\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))-\mathcal{P}m\mathbf{\overline{U}}(t)&\ni\boldsymbol{0},\\
\boldsymbol{z}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\end{aligned}}}\right.

where we assume
\mathbf{\overline{U}}(\cdot)
is a distributed control in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega))
. Let us take the inner product with
\boldsymbol{w}_{k},\ k=0,\ldots,n
in the first equation of the system (
5.8
) to get

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)+\mu(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{w}_{k})+\alpha(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{w}_{k})

(5.9)

\displaystyle=-(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{w}_{k})-\beta(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{w}_{k})-\gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{w}_{k})+(m\mathbf{\overline{U}}(t),\boldsymbol{w}_{k}),

where we have used the fact that
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
in the elements of
\mathcal{K}=\partial\mathcal{K}
. We have

(5.10)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{w}_{k})=(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{k})-(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{k})=b(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})+b(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})+b(\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w}_{k}),

and, by Taylor’s formula (
[
32
, Theorem 7.9.1]
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z})=\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\boldsymbol{z}+\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{z}\otimes\boldsymbol{z})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta.

It implies that

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{w}_{k})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{z}\otimes\boldsymbol{z})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)

(5.11)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})+\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{k}z_{i}z_{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right),

where
z_{k}(t):=(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{w}_{k})
for
k=0,\ldots,n
. Then the equation (
5.2
) together with (
5.10
)-(
5.2
) yields

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} z_{k}^{\prime}(t)+\mu\lambda_{k}z_{k}(t)+\alpha z_{k}(t)+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}h_{ik}^{1}z_{i}(t)&+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}h_{ik}^{2}z_{i}(t)+\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ijk}^{1}z_{i}(t)z_{j}(t)+\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ijk}^{2}(\boldsymbol{z})z_{i}(t)z_{j}(t)\\
&=(m\mathbf{\overline{U}}(t),\boldsymbol{w}_{k}),\\
z_{k}(0)&=(\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{w}_{k}),\end{aligned}}}\right.

where

\displaystyle g_{ijk}^{1}

\displaystyle=b(\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{j},\boldsymbol{w}_{k}),

\displaystyle g_{ijk}^{2}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)+\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right),

\displaystyle h_{ik}^{1}

\displaystyle=b(\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})+b(\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})\text{ and }h_{ik}^{2}=\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})+\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{k}),

for
i,j,k=0,\ldots,n
. Note that (
5.12
) represents a system of
n
nonlinear ordinary differential equations with unknowns
z_{k}(\cdot)
, for
k=0,\ldots,n
. We define the linear operator
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{N}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\boldsymbol{v}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu\lambda_{k}z_{k}+\alpha z_{k}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(h_{ik}^{1}+h_{ik}^{2})z_{i}}}\right)_{k=1}^{n},\ \mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ijk}^{1}z_{i}z_{j}}}\right)_{k=1}^{n}

(5.13)

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ijk}^{2}(\boldsymbol{z})z_{i}z_{j}}}\right)_{k=1}^{n}.

where
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(z_{k}(\cdot)}}\right)_{k=1}^{n}
. Also, we define
\mathcal{H}:\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2}(\omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
\mathcal{H}\overline{\mathbf{U}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((m\mathbf{\overline{U}},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})}}\right)_{k=1}^{n}
.
We finally rewrite the system (
5.12
) in the following matrix form:

(5.14)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}(t)+\mathcal{L}\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))+\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))&=\mathcal{H}\overline{\mathbf{U}}(t),\ \text{ for a.e. }\ t>0,\\
\boldsymbol{v}(0)&=\boldsymbol{v}_{0},\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(z_{k}(0)}}\right)_{k=1}^{n}.

Step 2.

The linear system and stabilization:

We linearize the above system (
5.14
) about
\boldsymbol{0}
as

(5.15)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}(t)+\mathcal{L}\boldsymbol{v}(t)&=\mathcal{H}\overline{\mathbf{U}}(t),\ \text{ for a.e. }\ t>0,\\
\boldsymbol{v}(0)&=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

Moreover, we write the corresponding adjoint system as

(5.16)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}^{\prime}(t)-\mathcal{L^{*}}\boldsymbol{q}(t)=0,\ \text{ for a.e. }\ t>0,

where
\mathcal{L^{*}}
is adjoint to
\mathcal{L}.
We will now prove the unique continuation property of the adjoint system (
5.16
). In order to do this, we need to show that
\mathcal{H}^{*}\boldsymbol{q}(t)=0
for all
t>0
implies
\boldsymbol{q}(t)=0
, for all
t>0
, where
\mathcal{H}^{*}(\cdot)
is adjoint to
\mathcal{H}(\cdot)
(see
[
53
, pp. 762]
). Then, for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we calculate

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\overline{U}},\mathcal{H}^{*}\boldsymbol{v})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega)}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{H}\mathbf{\overline{U}},\boldsymbol{v})_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{H}\mathbf{\overline{U}}}}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}=(m\mathbf{\overline{U}},\boldsymbol{w}_{1})z_{1}+\cdots+(m\mathbf{\overline{U}},\boldsymbol{w}_{n})z_{n}

\displaystyle=(m\mathbf{\overline{U}},\boldsymbol{w}_{1}z_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{w}_{n}z_{n})=\bigg(m\mathbf{\overline{U}},\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{w}_{k}z_{k}\bigg)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\bigg(\mathbf{\overline{U}},\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}z_{k}\boldsymbol{w}_{k}\bigg)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega)},

for all
\mathbf{\overline{U}}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega)
. This gives that
\mathcal{H}^{*}\boldsymbol{v}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}z_{k}\boldsymbol{w}_{k}|_{\omega}.
So, if
\boldsymbol{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}),
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{*}\boldsymbol{q}(t)=0\ \text{ if and only if }\ \sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}q_{k}(t)\boldsymbol{w}_{k}|_{\omega}=0\ \text{in}\ \mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega).

Since
\{\boldsymbol{w}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we know that
\{\boldsymbol{w}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega)
is linearly independent, which shows that
q_{k}(t)=0
for all
t>0
and
k=1,2,\ldots,n
(see
[
24
, pp. 1458]
). This proves the unique continuation property for the adjoint system (
5.16
) and hence the system (
5.15
) is exactly controllable (see
[
28
, Chapter 2]
). Thus by the well known
Wonham theorem

[
70
, Chapter 2, pp. 35]
, one can conclude that (
5.15
) is
completely stabilzable
. Therefore by definition, it asserts that there exists a linear and continuous feedback controller
\mathrm{R}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{L}^{2}(\omega)
and a constant
\hat{M}>0
such that for every
\sigma>0,
the solution of (
5.15
) satisfies
|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\hat{M}e^{-\sigma t}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},
for
t>0
, which can also be written as

(5.17)

\displaystyle|e^{-(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{H}\mathrm{R})t}|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\hat{M}e^{-\sigma t},\ \text{for}\ t>0,

where
\mathscr{L}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the space of all bounded linear operators on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover from (
5.15
), we infer that the controller
\mathbf{\overline{U}}(t)=\mathrm{R}\boldsymbol{v}(t)
stabilzes the following system asymptotically:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}(t)+(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{H}\mathrm{R})\boldsymbol{v}(t)&=\boldsymbol{0},\ \text{ for a.e. }\ t>0,\\
\boldsymbol{v}(0)&=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

Step 3.

Stabilization of the nonlinear system:

Let us now consider the following nonlinear system corresponding to (
5.14
):

(5.18)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}(t)+(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{H}\mathrm{R})\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))+\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))&=\boldsymbol{0},\ \text{ for a.e. }\ t>0,\\
\boldsymbol{v}(0)&=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

Our aim is to now show that the controller
\mathbf{\overline{U}}(t)=\mathrm{R}\boldsymbol{v}(t)
stabilizes the nonlinear system (
5.18
) exponentially. The solution of (
5.18
) is given by the variation of constant formula

(5.19)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}(t)=e^{-(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{H}\mathrm{R})t}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{H}\mathrm{R})(t-s)}\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{v}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s-\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{H}\mathrm{R})(t-s)}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v}(s))\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

Using (
5.17
) in (
5.19
) to obtain

(5.20)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\hat{M}e^{-\sigma t}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\hat{M}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\sigma(t-s)}|\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{v})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\hat{M}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\sigma(t-s)}|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

From (
5.2
), we have

(5.21)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ijk}^{1}z_{i}z_{j}}}\right)^{2}.

By using (
2.1
), we calculate

\displaystyle|g_{ijk}^{1}|=|b(\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{j},\boldsymbol{w}_{k})|\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x)||\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{j}(x)||\boldsymbol{w}_{k}(x)|\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{2\pi}{\mathrm{L}}|\mathbb{T}^{d}|,

which gives
|g_{ijk}^{1}|^{2}\leq\frac{32\pi^{2}}{\mathrm{L}^{d+2}}.
On employing discrete Hölder’s inequality in (
5.21
), we find

\displaystyle|\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\frac{32\pi^{2}}{\mathrm{L}^{d+2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}z_{i}z_{j}}}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{32\pi^{2}}{\mathrm{L}^{d+2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{2}}}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{32\pi^{2}}{\mathrm{L}^{d+2}}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{4},

which implies that

(5.22)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{v})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\gamma_{0}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},

where the constant
\gamma_{0}:=\frac{4\pi}{\mathrm{L}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Now we calculate
|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
separately for
r=3
with
\gamma=0,
and
r>3
and
q\in[3,r)
.

Step 4.

The case
r=3
with
\gamma=0.
Using discrete Hölder’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ijk}^{2}(\boldsymbol{z})z_{i}z_{j}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\beta^{2}\sum\limits_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{2}}}\right)^{2}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\leq\beta^{2}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{4}\sum\limits_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)^{2}.

We now calculate

(5.24)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta}}\right)\boldsymbol{w}_{k}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x}}\right|.

Using (
2.1
) and (
2.4
) (for
r=3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta}}\right|

\displaystyle=2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(((\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{i})\boldsymbol{w}_{j}+((\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\boldsymbol{w}_{i}+(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{j})}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 6|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z}||\boldsymbol{w}_{i}||\boldsymbol{w}_{j}|.

Thus from (
5.24
) and (
2.1
), we infer

(5.25)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 6\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|+|\boldsymbol{z}(x)|)\/\mathrm{d}\/x.

Let us calculate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{z}(x)|\/\mathrm{d}\/x=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}z_{k}\boldsymbol{w}_{k}(x)}}\right|\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}|z_{k}||\boldsymbol{w}_{k}(x)|}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq|\boldsymbol{v}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{w}_{k}(x)|\/\mathrm{d}\/x}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{n}\mathrm{L}^{\frac{d}{2}}|\boldsymbol{v}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}.

Thus by the above estimate, (
5.25
) implies that

(5.26)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 6\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C_{1}+\sqrt{n}\mathrm{L}^{\frac{d}{2}}|\boldsymbol{v}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}}\right),

where
\|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}\leq C_{1}
(see (
B.3
) below). Using (
5.26
), we obtain from (
5.2
) that

(5.27)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{1}(1+|\boldsymbol{v}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},

where
\gamma_{1}:=6\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{C_{1},\sqrt{n}\mathrm{L}^{\frac{d}{2}}}}\right\}
. Combining (
5.22
) and (
5.27
), and using it in (
5.20
), we find

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\hat{M}e^{-\sigma t}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\hat{M}\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\sigma(t-s)}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\hat{M}\gamma_{1}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\sigma(t-s)}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{3}\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

where
\gamma_{0}^{\prime}=\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{1}.
Using the Gronwall lemma, the above inequality implies

(5.28)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\hat{M}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\sigma t+\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{t}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\gamma_{1}\int_{0}^{t}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right),

for
t>0.
Let us suppose that the initial data
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
be such that
\hat{M}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho
for some constant
\rho>0,
which will be specified later. We want to show that
|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho
for all
t>0
. Suppose this does not hold true. Then by the continuity of
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
, there exists a time
T_{1}>0
such that

(5.29)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho\ \text{for}\ t\in[0,T_{1})\ \text{and}\ |\boldsymbol{v}(T_{1})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\rho.

For
t=T_{1}
, (
5.28
) implies that

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(T_{1})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\hat{M}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\sigma+\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\rho+\gamma_{1}\rho^{2}}}\right)T_{1}}}\right\}.

It can be easily seen that the quadratic equation
-\sigma+\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\rho+\gamma_{1}\rho^{2}=0
has positive discriminant, so it has real roots. Even, one can conclude that for
\rho<\rho_{1}:=-\frac{\gamma_{0}^{\prime}}{2\gamma_{1}}+\sqrt{\frac{\sigma}{\gamma_{1}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\gamma_{0}^{\prime}}{2\gamma_{1}}}}\right)^{2}}
, where
\rho_{1}>0
, we have
|\boldsymbol{v}(T_{1})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho,
which is a contradiction to (
5.29
). Thus, we have

(5.30)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho\ \text{for every}\ t\in[0,\infty).

Hence, from (
5.28
) and (
5.30
), we conclude that
|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\hat{M}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\delta_{1}t},
for every
t\geq 0,
where
\delta_{1}=\sigma-\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\rho-\gamma_{1}\rho^{2}>0
, as we know
-\sigma+\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\rho+\gamma_{1}\rho^{2}<0
for all
\rho\in(0,\rho_{1})
. Then for
\delta\in(0,\delta_{1})
, the above estimate implies

(5.31)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}e^{\delta t}|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=0.

But
\delta
is arbitrary in
(0,\sigma),
if
\rho
is small enough. Since
\sigma>0
is also arbitrary, then
\delta
is arbitrary in
(0,\infty).

Hence, for the feedback controller
\mathbf{\overline{U}}(t)=\mathrm{R}\boldsymbol{v}(t)
and sufficiently small initial data, the solution
\boldsymbol{v}(t)
of (
5.14
) is defined for all
t\geq 0
and the stability condition (
5.31
) holds. Furthermore, as
\boldsymbol{z}(t)\in\mathcal{K}
for
t\in[0,\infty)
, the solution
\boldsymbol{z}(t)
of (
5.8
) is defined on
[0,\infty)
, for the feedback controller
\mathbf{\overline{U}}(t)=\mathrm{R}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{z}(t)
, and the exponential stability condition
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}e^{\delta t}|\boldsymbol{z}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=0
holds.

Step 5.

The case
r>3
and
q\in[3,r)

and

\gamma<0
.
In this case, we find

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ijk}^{2}(\boldsymbol{z})z_{i}z_{j}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\bigg[\beta\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)z_{i}z_{j}+\gamma\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)z_{i}z_{j}\bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)z_{i}z_{j}}}\right)^{2}

(5.32)

\displaystyle\quad+2\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)z_{i}z_{j}}}\right)^{2}=:2\beta^{2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}E_{k}^{1}+2\gamma^{2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}E_{k}^{2}.

We calculate
E_{k}^{1}
by using discrete Hölder’s inequality as follows:

\displaystyle E_{k}^{1}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)z_{j}}}\right)z_{i}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{2}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}^{2}}}\right).

In a similar way, we can estimate
E_{k}^{2}
also. Using the above estimates, we obtain from (
5.2
) that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta^{2}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{4}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum\limits_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)^{2}

(5.33)

\displaystyle\quad+2\gamma^{2}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{4}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum\limits_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)^{2}.

Moreover, we calculate

(5.34)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta}}\right)\boldsymbol{w}_{k}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x}}\right|.

From (
2.4
), we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq(r-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{1}\hskip-5.69054pt|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z}|^{r-3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(((\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{i})\boldsymbol{w}_{j}+((\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\boldsymbol{w}_{i}+(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{j})}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta}}\right|

\displaystyle\quad+(r-1)(r-3)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{1}|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z}|^{r-5}((\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{i})((\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{j})(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 3(r-1)|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z}|^{r-2}|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}||\boldsymbol{w}_{j}|+(r-1)(r-3)|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z}|^{r-2}|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}||\boldsymbol{w}_{j}|

(5.35)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{r-3}r(r-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-2}+|\boldsymbol{z}|^{r-2}}}\right)|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}||\boldsymbol{w}_{j}|.

Using (
5.2
) and (
2.1
) in (
5.34
), we get

(5.36)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)}}\right|\leq 2^{r-3}r(r-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-2}+|\boldsymbol{z}|^{r-2}}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/x.

Now, we calculate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{z}(x)|^{r-2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg|\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}z_{k}\boldsymbol{w}_{k}\bigg|^{r-2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}|\boldsymbol{w}_{k}(x)|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-2}\bigg(\frac{2n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}\bigg)^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\mathrm{L}^{d}.

Thus by the Sobolev embedding and the above inequality, we obtain from (
5.36
)

(5.37)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)}}\right|\leq 2^{r-3}r(r-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg(C_{2}+|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-2}\bigg(\frac{2n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}\bigg)^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\mathrm{L}^{d}\bigg),

where
\|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r-2}}^{r-2}\leq C_{2}
(see (
B.3
) below). Similarly, one can determine

(5.38)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\theta\boldsymbol{z})(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{w}_{j})\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta,\boldsymbol{w}_{k}}}\right)}}\right|\leq 2^{r-3}r(r-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg(C_{3}+|\boldsymbol{v}|^{q-2}\bigg(\frac{2n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}\bigg)^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\bigg),

where
\|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{q-2}}^{r-2}\leq C_{3}
(see (
B.3
) below). Using (
5.37
)-(
5.38
), we deduce from (
5.2
) that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{r-3}r(r-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta\bigg(C_{2}+|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r-2}\bigg(\frac{2n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}\bigg)^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\mathrm{L}^{d}\bigg)|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle+2^{r-3}r(r-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}|\gamma|\bigg(C_{3}+|\boldsymbol{v}|^{q-2}\bigg(\frac{2n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}\bigg)^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\bigg)|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},

which implies

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{v})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\gamma_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+|\boldsymbol{v}|^{q}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}}\right),

where
\gamma_{2}:=2^{r-2}r(r-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\max\bigg\{\beta C_{2}+|\gamma|C_{3},\beta\bigg(\frac{2n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}\bigg)^{\frac{r-2}{2}},|\gamma|\bigg(\frac{2n}{\mathrm{L}^{d}}\bigg)^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\bigg\}.
Therefore, from (
5.17
) and (
5.18
), we obtain

(5.39)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\hat{M}e^{-\sigma t}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\hat{M}\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\sigma(t-s)}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\hat{M}\gamma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\sigma(t-s)}(|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{r}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{q}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

where
\gamma_{0}^{\prime}=\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{2}.
We calculate by using Young’s inequality as

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}|^{q}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{q}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})^{\frac{r-q}{(r-1)q}}(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{q}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})^{\frac{r(q-1)}{q(r-1)}}\leq C_{4}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{r}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\frac{\sigma}{4\gamma_{2}}|\boldsymbol{v}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},

where
C_{4}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{4\gamma_{2}}{\sigma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}\right)}}\right]^{\frac{r-q}{q-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q-1}{r-1}}}\right).
Similarly, we calculate

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})^{\frac{r-3}{2(r-1)}}(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})^{\frac{r+1}{2(r-1)}}\leq C_{5}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{\frac{r+1}{2}}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\frac{\sigma}{4\gamma_{0}^{\prime}}|\boldsymbol{v}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},

where
C_{5}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{\prime}}{\sigma}\frac{(r-3)}{(r-1)}}}\right]^{\frac{r-3}{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{r-1}}}\right)
. Using the above estimates in (
5.39
), we get

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\hat{M}e^{-\sigma t}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\hat{M}\gamma_{0}^{\prime}C_{5}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\sigma(t-s)}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{\frac{r+1}{2}}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle\quad+\hat{M}(1+C_{4})\gamma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\sigma(t-s)}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{r}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\frac{\hat{M}\sigma}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\sigma(t-s)}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

Now by applying the Gronwall inequality, we obtain

(5.40)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\hat{M}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{\sigma}{2}t+(1+C_{4})\gamma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{r-1}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s+\gamma_{0}^{\prime}C_{5}\int_{0}^{t}|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}}\right),

for all
t>0
. Similar to the case
r=3
with
\gamma=0
, let us suppose that the initial data
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
is such that
M|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho
for some
\rho>0
. We want to then show that
|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho
for all
t>0.
Suppose it is not true. Then as
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
is continuous function, we can find some
T_{1}>0
such that

(5.41)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho\ \text{ for }\ t\in(0,T_{1})\ \text{ and }\ |\boldsymbol{v}(T_{1})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\rho.

Then from (
5.40
), we calculate

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}(T_{1})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq\hat{M}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{\sigma}{2}+(1+C_{4})\gamma_{2}\rho^{r-1}+\gamma_{0}^{\prime}C_{5}\rho^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}}\right)t}}\right\}.

We see that the quadratic equation
(1+C_{4})\gamma_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}}\right)^{2}+\gamma_{0}^{\prime}C_{5}\rho^{\frac{r-1}{2}}-\frac{\sigma}{2}=0
has a positive discriminant, so it has real roots. Thus for

\displaystyle\rho<\rho_{1}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{-\gamma_{0}^{\prime}C_{5}+\sqrt{(\gamma_{0}^{\prime}C_{5})^{2}+2(1+C_{4})\gamma_{2}\sigma}}{2(1+C_{4})\gamma_{2}}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{r-1}},

we have
|\boldsymbol{v}(T_{1})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho
, which contradicts (
5.41
). Thus, we conclude that
|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}<\rho
on
t\in(0,\infty).
Proceeding similar to the case
r=3
with
\gamma=0
, we can find the existence of
\delta>0
, arbitrarily chosen on
(0,\infty),
such that
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}e^{\delta t}|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=0.
Furthermore, the solution
\boldsymbol{z}(t)
of (
5.8
) is defined on
[0,\infty)
for the feedback controller
\mathbf{\overline{U}}(t)=\mathrm{R}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}\boldsymbol{z}(t)
, and the exponential stability condition
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}e^{\delta t}|\boldsymbol{z}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=0
holds.
∎

Appendix A
Supporting results

The purpose of this section is to introduce some basic results regarding the nonlinear operators appearing in the system (
3.5
).

Proposition A.1
.

Define the operator
\mathcal{M}(\cdot):\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{H}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\cdot)=\mu\mathrm{A}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)+\alpha\mathrm{I}+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\cdot)+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}_{2}}(\cdot),

where
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{M})=\{\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}:\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{H}\}.
Then, for
d=2,3
with
r>3
, the operator
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is
m
-accretive in
\mathbb{H}
with
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
for some sufficiently large
\kappa>0
.

Proof.

We shall first show that
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is a monotone operator for some sufficiently large
\kappa>0
. Then we prove that
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is coercive and demicontinuous, which imply the
m
-accretivity of the operator
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}
. Finally, we demonstrate that
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
, for some sufficiently large
\kappa>0
. The proof is divided into the following steps:

Step I:

The operator
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is monotone for some sufficiently large
\kappa>0
.
Let us define
\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}:=\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}
. We estimate
\langle\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{2},\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle
by using an integration by parts as

(A.1)

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{2},\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle=\|\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}.

Note that
\langle\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}),\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle=0
, which along with Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities implies

(A.2)

\displaystyle|\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle|=|\langle\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle|\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\mu}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we estimate the term
\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{2}|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{2}|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}(x)|^{\frac{4}{r-1}}|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}(x)|^{\frac{2(r-3)}{r-1}}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

(A.3)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon\beta\mu}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{r-3}{r-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{4}{\varepsilon\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right]^{\frac{2}{r-3}}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for
r>3
and
\varepsilon>0
. Using (
A
) in (
A.2
), we find

(A.4)

\displaystyle|\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle|\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\varrho_{\varepsilon}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\varrho_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{r-3}{2\mu(r-1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{4}{\varepsilon\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right]^{\frac{2}{r-3}}.

From (
2.10
), we can write

\displaystyle\beta\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\||\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right)

(A.5)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta}{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\||\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right)+\frac{\beta}{2^{r}}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}.

Using Taylor’s formula and (
2.4
) (for
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\cdot)
), we calculate

(A.6)

\displaystyle|\gamma\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle|\leq|\gamma|2^{q-2}q\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}+|\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1})|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x.

Using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}|^{2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q-1}{r-1}}}\right)}|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}(x)|^{2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{q-1}{r-1}}}\right)}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

(A.7)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\widetilde{\varepsilon}\beta}{2^{q}q|\gamma|}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\rho_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\rho_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}:=\frac{r-q}{r-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{2^{q}q|\gamma|(q-1)}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}\beta(r-1)}}}\right]^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}
and
\widetilde{\varepsilon}>0
. Similarly, one can calculate

(A.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq\frac{\varepsilon\beta}{2^{q}q|\gamma|}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\rho_{\varepsilon}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\rho_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{r-q}{r-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{2^{q}q|\gamma|(q-1)}{\varepsilon\beta(r-1)}}}\right]^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}
. Using (
A
)-(
A.8
) in (
A.6
), we write

(A.9)

\displaystyle|\gamma\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle|\leq\frac{\widetilde{\varepsilon}\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(\rho_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}+\rho_{\varepsilon})\|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Combining (
A.1
), (
A.4
)-(
A
) and (
A.9
), we conclude

\displaystyle\langle(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(\alpha+\kappa-(\varrho_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}+\rho_{\varepsilon}))\|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2^{r}}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}}\right)\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\widetilde{\varepsilon}}}\right)\||\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Choosing
0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
,
0<\widetilde{\varepsilon}\leq 1
and
\kappa\geq\varrho_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}+\rho_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}\rangle\geq 0.

Thus the operator
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is monotone for any
\kappa\geq\varrho_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}+\rho_{\varepsilon}
.

Step II:

The operator
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is demicontinuous and coercive.
One can show that the operator
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}:\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}\to\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}
is demicontinuous and coercive in a similar way as in the proof of
[
38
, Proposition 3.1]
.

Step IV:

\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
for sufficiently large
\kappa>0
. Note that the space
\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
is reflexive. Since the operator
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is monotone, hemicontinuous and coercive from
\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
to
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}
, then by an application of
[
30
, Example 2.3.7]
, we obtain that the operator
\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is maximal monotone in
\mathbb{H}
with domain
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})\supseteq\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
for any
\kappa\geq\varrho_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}+\rho_{\varepsilon}
.

Let us define

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{y}):=\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{y})+\kappa\boldsymbol{y}=\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y})+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y})+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y})+\kappa\boldsymbol{y},

with
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{S})=\{\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}:\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y})+(\alpha+\kappa)\boldsymbol{y}+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y})+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y})\in\mathbb{H}\}
. Let us take the inner product in
\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{y})
with
\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})
to get

\displaystyle\mu\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+(\alpha+\kappa)\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{y}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))-(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))-\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+(\mathscr{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})),

where
\mathscr{S}(\cdot):=\mu\mathrm{A}+\mathcal{B}(\cdot)+\beta\mathcal{C}_{1}(\cdot)+\gamma\mathcal{C}_{2}(\cdot)+(\alpha+\kappa)\mathrm{I}.
Note that

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq C\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\ \text{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{y}_{e}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}).

A calculation similar to (
A.4
) gives

(A.10)

\displaystyle|(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))|\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\eta_{1}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\eta_{1}:=\frac{r-3}{2\mu(r-1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{4}{\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right]^{\frac{2}{r-3}}.
We now calculate by using Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x))|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

(A.11)

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{r-1}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x))|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{r-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x))|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x}}\right)^{\frac{r-q}{r-1}}.

Thus, from (
1.6
), (
A
), the identity
\nabla|\boldsymbol{y}|^{k}=k\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}y_{j}\nabla y_{j}|\boldsymbol{y}|^{k-2}
, and Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we calculate

\displaystyle|\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))|

\displaystyle\leq|\gamma|\||\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{q-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+4|\gamma|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{q-1}{(q+1)^{2}}}}\right]\|\nabla|\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{q+1}{2}}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq q|\gamma|\||\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{2(q-1)}{r-1}}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{2(r-q)}{r-1}}

(A.12)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\eta_{2}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\eta_{2}:=(q|\gamma|)^{\frac{r-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{4}{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q-1}{r-1}}}\right)}}\right]^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}}\right).
From (
A.10
)-(
A
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(\alpha+\kappa-(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}))\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{y}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+(\mathscr{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})).

For any
\kappa\geq\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}
, in view of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we reach at

\displaystyle\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{y})\|_{\mathbb{H}}+C\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}},

which provides
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{S})=\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})\subseteq\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
.

Now, choose
\kappa
sufficiently large so that
\kappa\geq\max\{\varrho_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}+\rho_{\varepsilon},\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}\}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark A.2
.

For
d=r=3
, by the Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{V}\subset\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}
so that
\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}=\mathbb{V}
. We have from (
2.10
) that

(A.13)

\displaystyle\beta\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}\rangle\geq\frac{\beta}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Moreover, we calculate

(A.14)

\displaystyle|\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}\rangle|\leq\mu\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\mu}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Modifying the calculation (
A
) for
r=3
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(x)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq\frac{1}{2^{q}q|\gamma|\mu}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\widetilde{\rho}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\widetilde{\rho}_{1}:=\rho_{\frac{1}{\mu\beta}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2^{q-1}q|\gamma|\mu(q-1)}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{3-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3-q}{2}}}\right)
. Similarly from (
A.8
) for
r=3
, we infer

(A.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(x)+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}(x)-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}\right)}{2^{q}q|\gamma|}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\widetilde{\rho}_{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\widetilde{\rho}_{2}:=\rho_{\frac{1}{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}\right)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2^{q-1}q|\gamma|(q-1)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}\right)}}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{3-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3-q}{2}}}\right)
. Thus, for
r=3
, from (
A.13
)-(
A.15
), we conclude

\displaystyle\langle(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{1})-(\mathcal{M}+\kappa\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{2}),\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq(\kappa-(\widetilde{\rho}_{1}+\widetilde{\rho}_{2}))\|\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}\right)\||\boldsymbol{y}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

(A.16)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}\right)\||\boldsymbol{y}_{2}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|(\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{2})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

From (
A.2
), it is clear that for
\kappa\geq\widetilde{\rho}_{1}+\widetilde{\rho}_{2}
and
2\beta\mu>1,
the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{M}(\cdot)+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is globally monotone (in fact strongly). The other properties like demincontinuity, coercivity, etc. of the operator
\mathcal{M}(\cdot)+\kappa\mathrm{I}
for
d=r=3
can be established in a similar way as for
r>3
in Proposition
A.1
.

Proposition A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a subset of
\mathbb{H}
which satisfies Hypothesis
3.1
. Let
\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
be the indicator function defined in (
3.2
). Define the multivalued operator
\mathfrak{G}:\mathrm{D}(\mathfrak{G})\multimap\mathbb{H}
by

\mathfrak{G}(\cdot)=\mu\mathrm{A}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)+\alpha\mathrm{I}+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\cdot)+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\cdot)+\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)+\kappa\mathrm{I},

with the domain
