\mathrm{D}(\mathfrak{G})=\{\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{H}:\varnothing\neq\mathfrak{G}(\boldsymbol{y})\subset\mathbb{H}\}
, for
\kappa>0
. Then, for
r>3
in
d=2,3
and
d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
,
\mathfrak{G}
is a maximal monotone operator in
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}
for sufficiently large
\kappa>0
with
\mathrm{D}(\mathfrak{G})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathrm{D}(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

It has been shown in Proposition
A.1
that the operator
\mathcal{S}(\cdot)=\mu\mathrm{A}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)+\alpha\mathrm{I}+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\cdot)+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\cdot)+\kappa\mathrm{I}
is maximal monotone with domain
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{S})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
in
\mathbb{H}
, for sufficiently large
\kappa>0
. On the other hand, the subdifferential operator
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
is a maximal monotone operator in
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}
(see
[
13
, Theorem 2.1, pp. 62]
). Therefore
\mathfrak{G}
is the monotone operator as it is the sum of two monotone operators
\mathcal{S}(\cdot)
and
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
. But the sum of two maximal monotone operator is not maximal monotone always. In order to prove
\mathfrak{G}
is maximal monotone, we need to show that

(A.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}(\mathrm{I}+\mathfrak{G})=\mathbb{H}.

Let us first consider the equation

(A.18)

\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})+\kappa\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{f},

where
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}
is the Yosida approximation of
\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
(see (
3.4
)). We next show that the equation (
A.18
) has at most one solution (uniqueness) for any given
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
.

Uniqueness.
Let
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
be arbitrary but fixed.
Let us set
\boldsymbol{z}_{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{1}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{2}
, where
\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{1}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{2}
are any two solutions of (
A.18
). Then we have

\displaystyle(\alpha+\kappa+1)\boldsymbol{z}_{\lambda}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}_{\lambda}+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{1})-(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{2})

(A.19)

\displaystyle=-(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{2}))-\beta(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{2}))-\gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{2})).

Taking the inner product with
\boldsymbol{z}_{\lambda}
in (
A
) and using (
A.4
)-(
A
) and (
A.9
) for
\varepsilon=1,\ \widetilde{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{z}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(\alpha+\kappa+1-(\varrho_{1}+\rho_{\frac{1}{2}}+\rho_{1}))\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq 0,

where we have used the monotonicity of
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\cdot)
also. Thus, for any
\kappa\geq\varrho_{1}+\rho_{\frac{1}{2}}+\rho_{1}
, we deduce
\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{1}=\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}^{2}
and this completes the uniqueness of the Yosida approximated problem (
A.18
).

Uniform bounds of
\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}
.

Taking the inner product with
\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}
in (
A.18
), we obtain

\displaystyle(\alpha+\kappa+1)\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})+((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})

(A.20)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})-(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})-\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}).

From (
A.4
)-(
A
) and (
A.9
), we have

(A.21)

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{8}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\varrho_{\frac{1}{2}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

(A.22)

\displaystyle|\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{8}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(\rho_{\frac{1}{2}}+\rho_{1})\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

and

(A.23)

\displaystyle\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta}{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right)+\frac{\beta}{2^{r}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}.

From the monotonicity of
(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\cdot)
, we have

\displaystyle-((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})\leq-((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{0}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})\leq\|(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{0})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})(\boldsymbol{0})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}=0.

Therefore, using (
A.21
)-(
A.23
), and Cauchy-Schwarz and Young’s inequalities in (
A
), we conclude

(A.24)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha+\kappa+\frac{1}{2}-\varrho_{\frac{1}{2}}-\rho_{\frac{1}{2}}-\rho_{1}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2^{r}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for any
\kappa\geq\varrho_{\frac{1}{2}}+\rho_{\frac{1}{2}}+\rho_{1}
. We rewrite (
A.18
) as

\displaystyle(\alpha+\kappa+1)(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\mu\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\beta\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\gamma\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})

(A.25)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{f}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\mathscr{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}).

We now take the inner product in (
A
) with
\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
and rearrange the terms to obtain

\displaystyle(\alpha+\kappa+1)\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))

\displaystyle+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))+((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})+(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\mathscr{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})+(\alpha+\kappa+1)((\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\mu(\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})

(A.26)

\displaystyle\quad+(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}).

We consider the cases
r>3
for
d=2,3
and
d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
separately.

Case I:

r>3
for
d=2,3
.
The following estimates are calculated in a similar fashion as (
A.10
)-(
A
):

(A.27)

\displaystyle|(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\eta_{1}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

(A.28)

\displaystyle|(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{16}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\varrho_{\frac{1}{4}}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

(A.29)

\displaystyle|\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{16}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\eta_{2}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\varrho_{\frac{1}{4}}:=\frac{r-3}{2\mu(r-1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{16}{\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right]^{\frac{2}{r-3}}.
By using interpolation inequality and Remark
2.1
, we calculate

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}^{r}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{3(r+1)}}^{\frac{3(r-1)}{4}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{\frac{r+3}{4}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{3(r-1)}{2(r+1)}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{\frac{r+3}{4}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(A.30)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{16}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}}}\right)^{\frac{r+3}{r+7}}.

Similarly, applying interpolation inequality for
2\leq 2q\leq 3(r+1)
, we find

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2q}}^{q}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(A.31)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{16}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+C\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{3(r+1)-2q}{3(r+1)-3q+1}}.

Moreover, from
[
12
, Proposition 1.1, part (iv), pp. 183]
, we infer that

(A.32)

\displaystyle((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})\geq 0.

Combining (
A.27
)-(
A.32
) and (
A.24
), and using the Cauchy-Schwarz and Young’s inequalities, we conclude from (
A
) that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha+\kappa+1-\varrho_{\frac{1}{4}}-\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}}}\right)\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\mu}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{2}{\mu}\|\mathscr{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

(A.33)

\displaystyle\quad+C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{3(r+1)-2q}{3(r+1)-3q+1}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}}}\right)^{\frac{r+3}{r+7}}}}\right)\leq C,

where
C=C(\mu,|\gamma|,\beta,\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}},\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}})>0
is a constant.

Case II:

d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1.
Let
0<\theta<1.

By using the Cauchy-Schwarz and Young’s inequalities

\displaystyle|(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))|

\displaystyle\leq\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(A.34)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\mu\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\theta\mu}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Similarly, we can write

(A.35)

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})|

\displaystyle\leq 4\theta\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{16\theta\mu}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

For
r=3
, we write (
1.6
) as

(A.36)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))

\displaystyle=\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla|(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})|^{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Similarly for
r=3
from (
A.29
), we have

(A.37)

\displaystyle|\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))|\leq\frac{1}{16\theta\mu}\|(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\widetilde{\eta}_{1}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8\theta\mu|\gamma|q(q-1)}}\right)^{\frac{3-q}{q-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{3-q}{2}}}}\right).
Also, from (
A
)-(
A
), we have

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16\theta\mu}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+C\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}^{\frac{12}{5}},

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16\theta\mu}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+C\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{12-2q}{13-3q}}.

and

\displaystyle|(\boldsymbol{f}+\mathscr{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}))|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}+\mathscr{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(A.38)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1-\theta)\mu}{2}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2(1-\theta)\mu}\|\boldsymbol{f}+\mathscr{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
0<\theta<1
is chosen the same as in (
A
).
Using the estimates (
A
)-(
A
) in (
A
) and the fact that

((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})\geq 0,
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mu(1-\theta)}{2}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta-\frac{1}{2\theta\mu}}}\right)\||(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\|\nabla|(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})|^{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

(A.39)

\displaystyle\quad+(\alpha+\kappa+1-\widetilde{\eta}_{1})\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq C,

where
C=C(\mu,|\gamma|,\beta,\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}},\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}})>0
is a constant.

As
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}),
from (
A
) (for
r>3
with
\kappa\geq\varrho_{\frac{1}{4}}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}
) and (
A
) (for
r=3
with
\kappa\geq\widetilde{\eta}_{1}
and
2\beta\mu>1
), we conclude that the sequence
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda>0}
is a bounded sequence in
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
. For sufficiently large
\kappa>0
, using Hölder’s and Agmon’s inequalities (see
[
3
, Lemma 13.2]
), (
A
) (for
r>3
) and (
A
) (for
r=3
), we calculate

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|((\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(A.40)

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{1-\frac{d}{4}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{\frac{d}{4}}\leq C.

Moreover, using interpolation inequality, (
A.24
), (
A
) (for
r>3
), (
A
) (for
r=3
) and Remark
2.1
, we estimate

(A.41)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}^{r}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{\frac{r+3}{4}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{3(r+1)}}^{\frac{3(r-1)}{4}}\leq C.

Similarly, using interpolation inequality with
2\leq 2q\leq 3(r+1)
, one can obtain

(A.42)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2q}}^{q}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{3(r+1)-2q}{3(r+1)-2}}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{3(r+1)}}^{\frac{3(r+1)(q-1)}{3(r+1)-2}}\leq C.

Let us rewrite (
A.18
) as

(A.43)

\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}+\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})+(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})=\boldsymbol{f}.

Using (
A
) (for
r>3
), (
A
) (for
r=3
), (
A
)-(
A.42
) in (
A.43
), we deduce

(A.44)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq C\ \mbox{ and }\ \|(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq C,

where
C=C(\mu,|\gamma|,\beta,\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}},\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}})>0
is a constant.

Passing limit as
\lambda\to 0.
By making the use of the Banach-Alaoglu theorem (see
[
42
]
) and denoting the subsequences again by
\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}
, we have the following convergences from (
A
) (for
r>3
), (
A
) (for
r=3
) and (
A.44
):

(A.45)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}&\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{y},\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{V},\\
\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}&\xrightharpoonup{w}\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y},\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H},\end{aligned}}}\right.\ \ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} (\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})&\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{f}_{1},\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H},\\
\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})&\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{f}_{2},\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

Moreover, by using the compact embedding
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}
, we have
\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\to\boldsymbol{y}
in
\mathbb{V}.

Finally, we pass the weak limit in (
A.43
) as
\lambda\to 0
to get
\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{f}_{1}+\boldsymbol{f}_{2}=\boldsymbol{f}
in
\mathbb{H}
.
Let us write (
A.43
) for
\lambda
and
\hat{\lambda}
, subtract and then take the inner product with
\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}}
to find

(A.46)

\displaystyle((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})-(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\hat{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}})+((\mathcal{S}+\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})-(\mathcal{S}+\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}})=0,

for all
\lambda,\hat{\lambda}>0.
By the monotonicity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot)+\mathrm{I}
, we obtain

(A.47)

\displaystyle((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})-(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\hat{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}})\leq 0.

Then by an application of
[
13
, Proposition 1.3, part (iv), pp. 49]
, we conclude from (
A.46
)-(
A.47
) that

(A.48)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{f}_{1})\in\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\ \text{and}\ \lim\limits_{\lambda,{\hat{\lambda}}\to 0}((\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})-(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})_{\hat{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}})=0.

Thus from (
A.46
), we deduce

(A.49)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\lambda,{\hat{\lambda}}\to 0}((\mathcal{S}+\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda})-(\mathcal{S}+\mathrm{I})(\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}}),\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\hat{\lambda}})=0.

Since the operator
\mathcal{S}(\cdot)+\mathrm{I}
is maximal monotone, (
A.45
), (
A.49
) and
[
13
, Lemma 1.3, pp. 49]
yield

(A.50)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{f}_{2})\in\mathcal{S}+\mathrm{I},

and it gives
\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{y})=\boldsymbol{f}_{2}
as
\mathcal{S}+\mathrm{I}
is a single-valued operator. This proves the surjectivity condition (
A.17
).

Now our aim is to show that
\mathrm{D}(\mathfrak{G})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathrm{D}(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}).

Note that
\mathfrak{G}=\mathcal{S}+\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
implies
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathrm{D}(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{S})\cap\mathrm{D}(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})\subseteq\mathrm{D}(\mathfrak{G})
. It follows from the surjectivity condition (
A.17
) that for any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
there exists a element
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathfrak{G})
such that
\boldsymbol{y}+\mathfrak{G}(\boldsymbol{y})\ni\boldsymbol{f}
. As we have seen in the previous step that
\boldsymbol{y}
can be approximated by a sequence of solutions
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda>0}
of equation (
A.18
). From (
A.48
) and (
A.50
), we infer that
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{S})\cap\mathrm{D}(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathrm{D}(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}})
and hence
\mathrm{D}(\mathfrak{G})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})\cap\mathrm{D}(\partial\mathrm{I}_{\mathcal{K}}).

∎

Appendix B
Stationary problem

Let us consider the abstract formulation of (
1.3
), by taking the Leray projection
\mathcal{P}
, which is given by

(B.1)

\displaystyle\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\beta\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})+\gamma\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})=\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d},

where
\boldsymbol{f}_{e}:=\mathcal{P}\boldsymbol{g}_{e}\in\mathbb{H}
is an external forcing.

Theorem B.1
.

For every
\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\in\mathbb{H}
, there exists at least one strong solution for the stationary problem (
). For

\displaystyle\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mu,\alpha}}\right\}\geq 2\mathfrak{K}_{2}+C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\beta\mu}\|\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mathfrak{K}_{1}}{\beta}|\mathbb{T}^{d}|}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
\mathfrak{K}_{1}:=|\gamma|^{\frac{r+1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2(q+1)}{\beta(r+1)}}}\right)^{\frac{r-q}{q+1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r+1}}}\right)
and
\mathfrak{K}_{2}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2^{q-1}q|\gamma|(q-1)}{\beta(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}\right)
, the solution is unique.

Proof.

Existence
:

From Proposition
A.1
, it is straightforward that the operator
\hat{\mathcal{M}}(\cdot):=\mu\mathrm{A}+\mathcal{B}(\cdot)+\alpha\mathrm{I}+\beta\mathcal{C}_{1}(\cdot)+\gamma\mathcal{C}_{2}(\cdot)
is
m
-accretive in
\mathbb{H}
for
r>3
in
d=2,3
and
d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
. Thus, from
[
12
]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}(\hat{\mathcal{M}}(\cdot)+\mathrm{I})=\mathbb{H},

which says that for every
\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\in\mathbb{H}
, there exists a
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})=\mathrm{D}(\hat{\mathcal{M}})
(Step IV in the proof of Proposition
A.1
) such that (
B.1
) is satisfied. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
is a strong solution to (
B.1
). We now derive some stationary energy estimates for the solution
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
of equation (
B.1
). Taking the inner product with
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
in (
B.1
), and then using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we obtain

(B.2)

\displaystyle\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathfrak{K}_{1}|\mathbb{T}^{d}|,

which implies that

(B.3)

\displaystyle\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mu,\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\right\}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{p}}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}\leq\frac{1}{2\mu}\|\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathfrak{K}_{1}|\mathbb{T}^{d}|,

where the constant
\mathfrak{K}_{1}:=|\gamma|^{\frac{r+1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2(q+1)}{\beta(r+1)}}}\right)^{\frac{r-q}{q+1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r+1}}}\right)
.

Uniqueness
:

Let us now establish the uniqueness of (
B.1
). Let
\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\boldsymbol{z}_{e}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
be two steady state solutions of (
B.1
) and define
\boldsymbol{w}_{e}:=\boldsymbol{y}_{e}-\boldsymbol{z}_{e}
. Then, from (
B.1
), we write

(B.4)

\displaystyle\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}+\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e})+\alpha\boldsymbol{w}_{e}+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}))+\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}))=\boldsymbol{0}.

By taking the inner product with
\boldsymbol{w}_{e}
in (
B.4
) and using (
2.10
), we obtain

(B.5)

\displaystyle\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e})\leq-(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e})-\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e}).

From Proposition
A.1
(see (
A
) and (
A.9
)), we have the following estimates:

\displaystyle|\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\||\boldsymbol{z}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\mathfrak{K}_{5}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

(B.6)

\displaystyle\beta(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\||\boldsymbol{z}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right),

where
\mathfrak{K}_{2}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2^{q-1}q|\gamma|(q-1)}{\beta(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}\right)
.
We calculate the bilinear term by using Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality as

(B.7)

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e})|=|(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{w}_{e},\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e})|\leq\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{z}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{z}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}.

Using (
B
)-(
B.7
) in (
B.5
), we find

\displaystyle\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mu,\alpha}}\right\}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\leq 2\mathfrak{K}_{2}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}+C\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{z}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}},

and it implies that

(B.8)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mu,\alpha}}\right\}-2\mathfrak{K}_{2}-C\|\nabla\boldsymbol{z}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\leq 0.

From (
B.2
), we calculate

\displaystyle\|\nabla\boldsymbol{z}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha\mu}\|\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\mathfrak{K}_{1}}{\mu}|\mathbb{T}^{d}|}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Thus, from (
B.8
), we conclude that
\boldsymbol{w}_{e}=0
and hence
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}=\boldsymbol{z}_{e}
. This completes the proof of uniqueness.
∎

Remark B.2
.

(1) We can also find

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e})|

\displaystyle\leq\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\mu}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}(x)|^{2}|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)-\boldsymbol{z}_{e}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle=\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\mu}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)|^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}(x)|^{r-1}+1}}\right)\frac{|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}(x)|^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}(x)|^{r-1}+1}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle\leq\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\mu}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}(x)|^{r-1}|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x+\frac{1}{4\mu}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,

where we used the fact that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}|^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}|^{r-1}+1}}}\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}<1
for
r\geq 3
.
By using Taylor’s formula, Hölder’s and Young’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle|\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e})|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{q-2}q|\gamma|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}+|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}(x)|^{q-1})|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{1}{4\mu}}}\right)\||\boldsymbol{z}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathfrak{K}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\mu}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathfrak{K}_{4}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\mathfrak{K}_{3}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2^{q-2}q|\gamma|}}\right)^{\frac{r-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2(q-1)}{(r-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{1}{4\mu}}}\right)}}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}
and
\mathfrak{K}_{4}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2^{q-2}q|\gamma|}}\right)^{\frac{r-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\mu(q-1)}{r-1}}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}
. Thus, from (
B.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle(\alpha-\mathfrak{K}_{3}-\mathfrak{K}_{4})\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{1}{4\mu}}}\right)\||\boldsymbol{z}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{1}{4\mu}}}\right)\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq 0.

Therefore, for
\alpha>\mathfrak{K}_{3}+\mathfrak{K}_{4}
and
\beta>\frac{1}{2\mu}
, we obtain
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}=\boldsymbol{z}_{e}
.

(2) We further calculate

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{w}_{e})|\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{z}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\varrho_{1}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\varrho_{1}:=\frac{r-3}{2\mu(r-1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{4}{\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right]^{\frac{2}{r-3}}.
Furthermore, by using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)|^{2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q-1}{r-1}}}\right)}|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)|^{2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{q-1}{r-1}}}\right)}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{2^{q-1}q|\gamma|}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\rho_{2}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\rho_{2}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2^{q-1}q|\gamma|(q-1)}{\beta(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}\right)
. Similarly, one can obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq\frac{\beta}{2^{q}q|\gamma|}\||\boldsymbol{z}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\rho_{1}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\rho_{1}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2^{q}q|\gamma|(q-1)}{\beta(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}\right)
. Using the above results, we find

\displaystyle|\gamma\langle\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{e}),\boldsymbol{y}_{e}-\boldsymbol{z}_{e}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{q-2}q|\gamma|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}(x)|^{q-1}+|\boldsymbol{z}_{e}(x)|^{q-1})|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{z}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2^{q-2}q|\gamma|(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2})\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Thus from (
B.5
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}(x)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq 2^{q-2}q|\gamma|(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2})\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\varrho_{1}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

It implies that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{\mu}{2},\alpha}}\right\}-2^{q-2}q|\gamma|(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2})-\varrho_{1}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{w}_{e}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\leq 0.

Therefore for
\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{\mu}{2},\alpha}}\right\}>2^{q-2}q|\gamma|(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2})+\varrho_{1}
, we get the uniqueness
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}=\boldsymbol{z}_{e}
.

Remark B.3
.

In all of the above cases, we observe that the linear damping term
\alpha\boldsymbol{y}
is helping us to obtain the existence and uniqueness result. For the non-zero average condition in the torus, without this term, the uniqueness of the stationary CBFeD model is an open problem. Thus the linear damping term plays a crucial role in order to obtain the solvability of the stationary system as well as the stability results.

Appendix C
Proportional Controllers

We are now going to discuss the stabilization via
proportional controllers
where the control is localized in an open subset
\Omega
of a torus having smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
. By a
proportional control
, we mean a linear feedback control
\boldsymbol{u}
which is proportional to the difference between the desired state (eqilibrium solution
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
) and actual state (solution
\boldsymbol{y}
). Consider the following controlled CBFeD system:

(C.1)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\partial\boldsymbol{y}}{\partial t}-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{y}+(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+\beta|\boldsymbol{y}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}+\gamma|\boldsymbol{y}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}+\nabla p&=\boldsymbol{f}_{e}+m\boldsymbol{u}(t),\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times(0,\infty),\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{y}&=0,\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}\times(0,\infty),\\
\boldsymbol{y}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\ &&\text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d},\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
and
m
is the characteristic function on
\Omega.
Moreover, here
\boldsymbol{u}=-k(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\ k\in\mathbb{N}
is a proportional control having support in
\Omega
. Let
(\boldsymbol{y}_{e},p_{e})\in\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times(\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
be the the steady state solution of the system (
C.1
) satisfying

(C.2)

-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\beta|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\gamma|\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{q-1}\boldsymbol{y}_{e}+\nabla p_{e}=\boldsymbol{f}_{e},\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{T}^{d}.

Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}:=\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus\ \overline{\Omega}
and define
\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}:=\{\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\infty}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}};\mathbb{R}^{d}):\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{y}=0\}.
We also define the Hilbert spaces
\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
as the closures of
\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}
in the Lebesgue space
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the Sobolev space
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, respectively. We identify the dual of
\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}
with itself and we define the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}}
in
\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}
as below

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}}:=\int_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}}\boldsymbol{y}(x)\boldsymbol{z}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x.

Let us now define the operator
\mathcal{A}:=-\mu\mathcal{P}\Delta\boldsymbol{y}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}
on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}
with the domain
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{A}):=\mathrm{H}^{2}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}.
Let us denote the first eigenvalue of an operator
\mathcal{A}
by
\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})
. Then using the concept of Rayleigh quotient, we know that

(C.3)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}):=\inf\{\mu\|\nabla\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}}^{2}+\alpha\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}}^{2}:\ \widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{V}},\ \|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}}=1\}.

It is clear that
\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})>0
. We now give an auxiliary result which will be used in the sequel.

Proposition C.1
.

Let us define the operator

\displaystyle\mathrm{A}_{k}\boldsymbol{y}:=\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+k\mathcal{P}(m\boldsymbol{y}),\ \text{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V},

with
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}_{k})=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
, where
k
is any positive real number. Then for each
\varepsilon>0,
there exists
k_{1}=k_{1}(\varepsilon)
such that

\displaystyle(\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+k\mathcal{P}(m\boldsymbol{y}),\boldsymbol{y})\geq(\mu\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})-\varepsilon)\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}
and
k\geq k_{1}
, where
\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})
is given by (
C.3
).

Remark C.2
.

Let
\nu_{k}^{1}
be the first eigenvalue of
\mathrm{A}_{k}
. Then, we have

(C.4)

\displaystyle\nu_{k}^{1}:=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+k\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x;\ \boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V},\ \|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}=1}}\right\}.

Let us define the following subsets of
\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{G_{1}}

\displaystyle=\{\mu\|\nabla\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}}^{2}+\alpha\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}}^{2}:\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{V}},\ \|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}}=1\}\ \text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathrm{G_{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+k\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x:\ \boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V},\ \|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}=1}}\right\}.

Let us consider
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
and extend by zero across the boundary
\partial\Omega
to
\mathbb{V}
. That is, we define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\ &\text{ in }\ \widetilde{\mathrm{Q}},\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\text{ in }\ \Omega.\end{cases}

Therefore, it is immediate that
\mathrm{G_{1}}\subset\mathrm{G_{2}},
since
\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}=\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}}=1
and

\displaystyle\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+k\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{y}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

\displaystyle=\mu\|\nabla\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}}^{2}+\alpha\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}}^{2}.

Hence, we conclude

(C.5)

\displaystyle\nu_{k}^{1}\leq\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}).

Proof of Proposition
C.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
be an eigenfunction of the operator
\mathrm{A}_{k}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\nu_{k}^{1}.
Then we write

(C.6)

\displaystyle\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}+k\mathcal{P}(m\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1})=\nu_{k}^{1}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}.

We may assume that the
\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}
is the normalized eigenfunction, that is,
\|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}\|_{\mathbb{H}}=1.
Then using (
C.5
), we get

(C.7)

\displaystyle\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+k\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x=\nu_{k}^{1}\leq\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}).

By using the Banach-Alaoglu theorem, we deduce from above that

(C.8)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}&\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{y}^{1}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{V},\\
\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}&\to\boldsymbol{y}^{1}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H},\end{cases}

where the final convergence in (
C.8
) is along a subsequence (denoted by the same notation) and due to the compact embedding
\mathbb{V}\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}
. Moreover, we have a convergent subsequence of positive reals
\nu_{k}^{1}\to\nu^{*}
. Thus from (
C.7
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\to 0\ \text{ as }\ k\to\infty.

By using the fact that
\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{V}}
, we get a subsequence
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{k_{l}}^{1}\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\{\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

(C.9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}_{k_{l}}^{1}\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{y}^{1}_{*}\ \text{ in }\ \widetilde{\mathbb{V}},

as
l\to\infty
. Since
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{k_{l}}^{1}\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
is a subsequence of
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, therefore from (
C.8
), we have

(C.10)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}_{k_{l}}^{1}\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{y}^{1}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{V},

The convergences (
C.9
),(
C.10
) and the fact
\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{V}}
yield
\boldsymbol{y}^{1}_{*}=\boldsymbol{y}^{1}
in
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
. From the strong convergence in
\mathbb{H}
, we conclude that
\|\boldsymbol{y}^{1}\|_{\mathbb{H}}=1
. Now, we multiply (
C.6
) by
\psi\in\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
and integrate over
\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}
to get

\displaystyle\mu\int_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}}\nabla\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}(x):\nabla\psi(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x+\alpha\int_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}}\boldsymbol{y}_{k}^{1}(x)\psi(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x=\nu_{k}^{1}\int_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}}\boldsymbol{y}^{1}_{k}(x)\psi(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x,

where we have used the fact that
m(x)=0
for
x\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}
. Using the convergences (
C.8
)-(
C.9
), we get
\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{y}^{1}=\nu^{*}\boldsymbol{y}^{1}
in
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
, so that
\nu^{*}
is an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{A}
. But from (
C.5
), we have
\nu^{*}\leq\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}),
and
\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})
is the first eigen value of
\mathcal{A}
. Therefore, it is immediate that
\nu^{*}=\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}),
and

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\nu_{k}^{1}=\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}).

This also says that for each
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
k_{1}=k_{1}(\varepsilon)>0
such that

\displaystyle|\nu_{k}^{1}-\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})|<\varepsilon\ \text{ for }\ k\geq k_{1}.

Thus by (
C.4
) and using the definition of
\mathrm{A}_{k}
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{y}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+k\mathcal{P}(m\boldsymbol{y}),\boldsymbol{y})\geq\nu^{1}_{k}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\geq(\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})-\varepsilon)\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

which completes the proof.
∎

Remark C.3
.

1.
It is known from the classical Rayleigh-Faber-Krahn perimetric inequality (see
[
17
, Section 5.4]
) in dimension
d\geq 2
that

(C.11)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\omega_{d}}{|\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}|}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}J_{\frac{d}{2}-1,1},

where
|\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}|
is the volume of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}
,
\omega_{d}
is the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
J_{m,1}
is the first positive zero of the Bessel function
I_{m}(r)
(see
[
6
]
).

2.
If
|\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}|
is sufficiently small (or
\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}
is sufficiently “thin”), then
\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})
becomes larger and larger.

Theorem C.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{f}_{e}\in\mathbb{H}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
be the solution of the equation (
C.2
). Also, let us set
\boldsymbol{z}(\cdot)=\boldsymbol{y}(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}.
Then, for
\boldsymbol{z}(0)\in\mathbb{H}
, the following problem
\text{for a.e.}\ t>0

(C.12)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}&+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\alpha\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))+k\mathcal{P}(m\boldsymbol{z}(t))=\boldsymbol{0},\ \\
\boldsymbol{z}(0)&=\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{y}_{e},\end{aligned}}}\right.

has unique weak solution for
r>3
and
r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
, satisfying

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})\ \text{ with }\ \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).

Furthermore, there exists a
\delta>0
such that the following global exponential stability condition holds:

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq e^{-\delta t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}},\text{ for all }t\in[0,T].

Proof.

Similar to the proof Proposition
A.3
, one can show that the operator
\mu\mathrm{A}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)+\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\cdot)+\alpha\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\cdot)+k\mathcal{P}(m\cdot)
is
m
-accretive in
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}.
Moreover, using the similar steps as we have done in the proof of Theorem
3.3
, one can prove that the system (
C.12
) has a unique weak solution

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})\ \text{ with }\ \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),

for
r>3
in
d=2,3
and
d=r=3
with
2\beta\mu>1
. We will prove only the stability part. Let us take the inner product with
\boldsymbol{z}(\cdot)
in (
C.12
) to get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mu\|\nabla\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\beta(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{z}(t))+k(\mathcal{P}(m\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{z}(t))

\displaystyle=-(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{z}(t))-\gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{z}(t)),

for a.e.
t\in[0,T].
Using the similar calculations as we have performed in Proposition
A.1
, we obtain

(C.13)

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{z}\rangle|\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\varrho^{*}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\varrho^{*}=\frac{r-3}{2\mu(r-1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{4}{\beta\mu(r-1)}}}\right]^{\frac{2}{r-3}}
. Furthermore, we have

(C.14)

\displaystyle|\gamma\langle\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{z}\rangle|\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\||\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+(\varrho_{1}^{*}+\varrho_{2}^{*})\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

where
\varrho_{1}^{*}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2^{q}q|\gamma|(q-1)}{2\beta(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}\right)
and
\varrho_{2}^{*}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2^{q}q|\gamma|(q-1)}{\beta(r-1)}}}\right)^{\frac{q-1}{r-q}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{r-q}{r-1}}}\right)
. From (
2.10
), one can deduce

(C.15)

\displaystyle\beta\langle\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e})-\mathcal{C}_{1}(\boldsymbol{y}_{e}),\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\rangle\geq\frac{\beta}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\||\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\||\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right).

Combining (
C.13
)-(
C.15
), we obtain from (
C.12
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mu}{2}\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\alpha\boldsymbol{z}(t)+k\mathcal{P}(m\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{z}(t)}}\right)\leq(\varrho^{*}+\varrho_{1}^{*}+\varrho_{2}^{*})\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for a.e.
t\geq 0.
Now, Proposition
C.1
and the above inequality imply

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})-\varepsilon}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq(\varrho^{*}+\varrho_{1}^{*}+\varrho_{2}^{*})\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

or

(C.16)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})-\varepsilon-\varrho^{*}-\varrho_{1}^{*}-\varrho_{2}^{*}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for a.e.
t\geq 0
. From Remark
C.3
, we see that if
|\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}}|
sufficiently small, then one can make the constant

\displaystyle\delta:=\lambda_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Q}})-\varepsilon-\varrho^{*}-\varrho_{1}^{*}-\varrho_{2}^{*}>0.

Thus from (
C.16
), it is immediate that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq e^{-2\delta t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

for all
t\geq 0.
This proves that the feedback control
\boldsymbol{u}(\cdot)
exponentially stabilizes the equilibrium solution
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}
of the system (
C.1
).
∎
